===== [5] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T19:49:19.108Z =====
Searched. One thing up front: **this container's network policy blocks every forum and TSB page** — f150forum, ford-trucks, fordf150.net, edmunds, reddit, static.nhtsa.gov all refused the connection. So I could run searches and read the search engine's summaries of those threads, but **I could not open a single thread myself**. Treat everything below as second-hand until you or I read the actual pages. What's marked "reported" is what the summaries said; nothing here is me confirming a page.

## The closest case I found to yours

A **2014 F-150 3.7L, RWD**: warm engine, at a full stop, **shakes and surges**, worse with A/C or defog on, idling **500–550 rpm** — owner's own theory was that idle speed was set too low. Two dealers, no fix. The second dealer **replaced the electronic throttle body assembly and it did not cure it.**

Two things matter for you there. First, the symptom set is documented on your exact engine and year, so you're not chasing a ghost. Second — and this is the useful part — **write down your actual idle rpm** in P, in D with A/C off, and in D with A/C on. If yours is also sagging into the 500s under load, the fault is idle-air/load control, not combustion, and it reframes the whole diagnosis. That's a free number and you don't need a scan tool for the tachometer.

Caveat I'll state plainly: **I found no thread where "less shake in D/R than in N" was the reported discriminator.** Almost every report is the opposite — worse in D. Your test result is still sound reasoning, it's just not corroborated by anything I found.

## What actually fixed it for other people

**1. Coil boots and plug gap — the top one for you.** Failing coil boots are reported as a very common misfire/rough-running cause on 2010–2016 F-150s: the rubber breaks down under heat, the spring loses tension, spark arcs to the head (carbon tracking). Ford TSB **14-0160** is cited for boots + gap check. And specifically: *"rough idle after spark plug change often results from incorrect gap or a damaged coil boot."*

You changed the plugs and the shake stayed. That's the profile. Pull the coils, look at each boot for tracking (a grey/black burn trail down the side), and **re-check the gap on the plugs you installed** — out-of-box gap is not guaranteed. Costs nothing but your time and it re-audits work already done.

**2. Oil in the spark plug wells.** Reported as a very common cause on the 3.7 Cyclone — valve cover gasket or plug tube seals leak, oil pools in the well, drowns the boot, kills the spark. You'll see it the moment you pull a coil.

**3. Cracked PCV hose or elbow.** Multiple owner reports of a rotted PCV elbow as *the* fix; one cited a **$4 part** after months of no-code rough idle. An unmetered vacuum leak downstream of the MAF is exactly a lean rough idle. Check the hose and elbow by hand with the engine running — squeeze and flex them.

**4. Throttle body carbon behind the plate.** Reported as preventing full plate closure and causing rough idle, cured by cleaning. This is still your cheapest unexplored lead, and it stays on the list — but note that in the 2014 3.7 case above, a **whole new throttle body** didn't fix that truck. So clean it and do the relearn, but don't bank the diagnosis on it.

**5. Your "cleaned" O2 sensors — this looks like real damage.** The sources are consistent: chemical cleaners degrade the platinum layer and the ceramic element, and the protective shroud's diffusion holes get wrecked. A lazy, slow-switching sensor is **platinum depletion — no chemical restores it**, and a slow sensor makes the PCM over-fuel. That's your rough idle, caused by the repair. Check upstream HO2S switching rate; if it's sluggish, replace, don't re-clean.

**6. CMCV / intake runner flaps (P2004–P2007).** Ford's redesigned manifolds have charge-motion control valve plates that stick with carbon buildup; codes P2004/5/6/7. FORScan can command the actuators and watch the position PIDs. Honest limits: the strongest documentation I saw is the **5.0L V8**, where P2006 usually means a broken plastic linkage inside the manifold. I did not confirm the failure rate on the 3.7.

**7. Cam phasers / timing chain stretch.** The 3.7 Ti-VCT has four phasers. Reported TSB method: watch **VCT_INT_ACT1 at idle — ≥ +6° means the chain is worn**; with the valve cover off, **more than 5 teeth of tensioner extension** = stretched. Phasers and tensioners need **≥ 25 psi at hot idle**. And the mechanism matches your case: rough idle from excess cylinder pressure because the cams are advanced when they shouldn't be. Given your odometer is "at least" 131,000 km, this is a live candidate and it's one FORScan PID to read.

**8. Your 1,000–2,000 rpm instability may be a separate fault.** 6R80 torque converter shudder is documented across 2007–2020 F-150, typically appearing hot (175–195°F+), commonly fixed by a **full fluid exchange** rather than a drain-and-fill. You did fluid at 113,000 km — worth knowing whether that was a pan drop or a true exchange. This one only shows up **in gear under light throttle at lockup**, never at a standstill, so it's easy to separate from the idle shake.

**9. Ignore this one unless it applies:** there's a 2014 F-150 3.7L long-block replacement program (**14B10**), but the summaries say it covers **gaseous-fuel-prep engines** only. Your VIN engine code M is the flex-fuel 3.7, not a CNG/LPG prep engine — so it almost certainly doesn't touch you. Not verified against the actual bulletin.

## What I'd do next, in order

Pull the six coils. Inspect boots for tracking, wells for oil, and gap every plug. Flex the PCV hose and elbow. Then clean the throttle body and run the relearn. That's one afternoon, no scan tool, and it covers the three most-reported fixes plus your own top lead.

Then the two numbers that end the guessing: **VCT_INT_ACT1 at hot idle** (≥ +6° condemns the chain) and **idle rpm in P vs D vs D-with-A/C** (a sag into the 500s changes the whole diagnosis).

===== [7] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T19:50:14.704Z =====
Good call. Let me narrow it before you touch anything. Four questions now, a second round after — each one kills a branch.

===== [8] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T19:54:50.649Z =====
Those four answers change the picture a lot. Let me state it plainly before the next round:

**Temperature-independent + load-independent + zero codes, ever + reproducible standing still in Park.** That combination argues *against* the repo's current conclusion that this is combustion. A misfire big enough to shake the cab almost always sets a code, and mixture faults behave differently in open loop (cold) than closed loop (hot). Yours doesn't care about temperature, doesn't care about A/C load, and never lit the lamp. What it *does* care about is rpm. That profile fits an **rpm-dependent mechanical or accessory vibration** — harmonic balancer, a belt-driven bearing, fan, or something as dumb as an exhaust heat shield touching the body.

It also gives your D-vs-N result a second explanation: shifting into gear rocks the engine on its mounts and drops idle rpm slightly — which moves a resonance off its peak, or moves a contacting part off what it's touching. Same observation, completely different cause.

Four more questions to settle it:

===== [9] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T19:57:37.108Z =====
That reverses what I said a moment ago: a needle that genuinely wanders at a held throttle is not imbalance. **Combustion is back on top** — the repo's original conclusion stands. Balance, mounts, damper, belt bearings and exhaust contact are all out, because none of them move a tachometer needle.

What the two rounds have actually bought you:

- **No code, ever, and the shake is equal at every rpm** → this is not one dead cylinder. The misfire monitor works by comparing crankshaft deceleration between cylinders, so a fault hitting all six *equally* can stay invisible to it. You're looking for a global fault, not a cylinder.
- **Identical cold and hot** → rules out the mixture faults that only exist in closed loop. Which means your "cleaned" O2 sensors are **not** the root cause — cold start runs open loop and ignores them, and you'd feel a difference. They may still be damaged and worth checking, but they're a side issue, not this.
- **Normal idle rpm + no change with A/C** → idle-air-control and low-idle-speed theories are dead, and that documented 2014 case with the 500 rpm sag is not your truck.
- **Present before all the repair work** → nothing you've replaced can be the cause. Wrong plugs, wrong gap, damaged O2 sensors can only be *added* faults on top.

That leaves a global fuelling or charge-control error: MAF drift, fuel delivery, EGR leaking, or the throttle plate itself wandering. Last round:

===== [10] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T20:02:57.445Z =====
That last answer cracks it. Let me put the pieces together, because they now form one clean pattern:

| Condition | Manifold vacuum | Shake |
|---|---|---|
| Park / Neutral, standstill | Highest | **Worst** |
| Drive / Reverse, standstill | Slightly lower | **Less** |
| Driving under load | Lowest | **Gone** |

**Rpm is not the variable. Load is.** You told me it was equal at all rpm in Park — but that whole sweep was at zero load. The moment there's real load on the engine it disappears completely, and your D-vs-N test sits exactly on that curve. The symptom is a strict function of manifold vacuum: high vacuum, shake; low vacuum, no shake.

That also means the repo's explanation of the D/N test — "the converter damps the pulses" — is the wrong mechanism. It's not damping. The engine genuinely runs better the moment it's loaded. Same observation, and it now points somewhere much more specific.

## What this eliminates

- **Fuel delivery** — pump, filter, pressure. A weak pump fails under load first. Yours pulls great. Out.
- **Mechanical: compression, valves, cam timing, phasers, timing chain.** All of these hurt more under load, not less. Out — and that takes the expensive end of the list off the table.
- **MAF** — largely out for the same reason, though low-flow drift is the one MAF fault that could survive this.
- **O2 sensors** — already out as root cause (identical cold, in open loop). Still possibly damaged, still a separate issue.
- **Ignition** — plugs changed nothing, and ignition faults worsen under load. Out.
- **Anything mechanical or rotating** — mounts, damper, flexplate, belt bearings, exhaust contact. Out; none of them wander a tach needle.

## What's left: an unmetered air / charge-dilution leak

Everything that fits is something whose effect scales with vacuum and vanishes at wide-open throttle:

1. **EVAP purge valve stuck partly open.** Leaks unmetered air and vapour into the manifold at idle, wanders the rpm, disappears under load, and very often sets no code. Cheap, commonly missed. Given it's been like this since you bought it — high on my list.
2. **Vacuum leak** — intake manifold gasket, PCV hose or elbow, brake booster hose. Same signature exactly.
3. **EGR valve leaking or not seating.** Dilutes the charge at light load; closes under heavy load, which is precisely when your truck goes smooth. Sets no code if the position sensor tracks the command.
4. **Throttle body carbon** — your existing lead, still alive, still cheap. Matters at small plate openings only.
5. **Injector spray quality at idle-length pulse widths** — fine at long pulses under load, poor at short ones. The clean didn't fix it, but it doesn't clear it either.

## Hand the workshop this, in this order

They have the tools, so use them on the two numbers the repo says you're blocked on:

**1. Fuel trims — LTFT and STFT, both banks, at three conditions:** warm idle in P, idle in D, and held 2,500 rpm no load. This one test sorts the whole list. Large positive LTFT at idle that **falls toward zero at 2,500 rpm** is an unmetered air leak, confirmed — that's the classic signature and it matches your load dependence exactly. Positive at *both* points to metering. Negative means rich, and turns the diagnosis around.

**2. Smoke test the intake tract.** Given the pattern, this is the highest-yield single test in the shop. Have them isolate the purge valve and PCV during the test so a leaking valve doesn't hide as "system leak."

**3. Pinch the purge line at idle.** Thirty seconds, no tools. If the idle steadies and the shake drops, you've found it. Also read the commanded purge duty at idle — it should be zero.

**4. EGR actual position at idle** — should read fully closed. Any opening at idle is your answer.

**5. Vacuum gauge on the manifold.** Steady 18–22 inHg at idle is healthy at your elevation. A needle that drifts or wanders across a range is mixture instability and corroborates the leak; a steady-but-low reading changes the story.

**6. Per-cylinder misfire counters.** Confirm what I'm predicting: all six roughly equal, no single outlier. If one cylinder stands out, I'm wrong about the global-fault reasoning and we go back to that cylinder.

**7. Read permanent DTCs, not just stored and pending.** Permanent codes survive a battery disconnect and a scan-tool clear. Since someone else has had this truck apart, that's the one place a cleared history can't hide.

Then clean the throttle body and run the idle relearn while it's open — but as housekeeping, not as the fix. Your truck's problem is load-dependent, and a dirty throttle body doesn't behave that cleanly.

One thing worth saying: you've spent money on plugs, filter, injector clean, fluids and O2 work, and none of it could ever have fixed this, because none of those faults get better under load. Get the trims read before anything else is bought.

===== [13] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T21:13:28.627Z =====
Net effect: **nothing in the diagnosis changes, and one thing gets firmer.** Here's the honest accounting.

**It isn't actually a contradiction.** "Less in D/R" was never "zero in D/R." The load model predicts exactly what your brother felt: manifold vacuum at idle in Drive is only a little lower than in Neutral, so the shake should be reduced but still clearly present. He's describing the middle row of that table, not a new symptom.

**D and R feeling the same is a genuine gain.** Engine torque reaction acts in *opposite* directions in Drive and Reverse. A collapsed or broken mount, or the engine touching something under torque, would feel different between them — that's the real mount test, better than the D-vs-N one the repo is built on. Identical in both means **mounts stay ruled out, on stronger evidence than before.**

**The needle wandering in gear too is expected, not new information.** Idle in Drive is still a high-vacuum, low-load condition — you're only loading the engine against a stalled converter, which is a fraction of road load. The instability spanning P, N, D and R at a standstill, then vanishing under real load on the road, is the same curve I described. If anything it tightens it.

**And I'm not moving anything on a "felt it once or twice" report.** You're right to keep the pattern question open. Second-hand impressions of vibration character are the least reliable data in this whole thread, and I'd rather have no answer than a wrong one anchoring the diagnosis.

If you want to settle it properly, it's a two-minute job and one you should do yourself: foot firmly on the brake, warm engine, A/C off, thirty seconds each in N, D and R, back to back in that order, watching the tach and paying attention to whether the *frequency* changes or only the *strength*. Frequency the same and only amplitude changing = load model confirmed, done. Frequency actually changing between N and D = something I'd need to rethink.

**Don't wait on that to act.** The truck is in a workshop with the tools right now, and that's the scarce resource here. Get the fuel trims read at those three conditions — idle in P, idle in D, 2,500 rpm no load — plus the smoke test and the EGR position at idle. Those numbers end the guessing; the D/R pattern question does not.

===== [14] USER 2026-09-02T21:14:54.264Z =====

===== [15] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T21:16:05.954Z =====
Straight answer, then the reasoning.

## My call: the engine is being diluted at idle and light load — most likely EGR not seating

Confidence: **moderate, not high.** I have no scan data, and I'm reasoning entirely from how the symptom moves with load. But if you made me commit to one thing to check first, it's the EGR valve leaking at idle.

**Why it fits better than anything else:**

- **It's a dilution fault, not a mixture fault.** EGR is inert gas. It makes combustion unstable without making it lean — so the O2 sensors still see roughly stoich, fuel trims stay near normal, and **no code ever sets.** You've owned this truck for years with a shake bad enough to feel in the cab and the lamp has never once come on. That single fact is the strongest thing you've told me, and it argues hard against a vacuum leak: a leak big enough to shake the truck would have driven long-term trim past +25% and set P0171/P0174 by now.
- **It explains the load curve exactly.** EGR fraction is large at idle, smaller with a bit of load in D/R, and trivially small under real road load — where the valve is closed anyway. That's your table, in order: worst in P/N, less in D/R, gone on the road.
- **It's all six cylinders equally**, which is why there's no misfire code — the monitor compares cylinders against each other and a fault that hits all of them evenly is close to invisible to it.
- **It's temperature-independent and load-symmetric**, which carbon on a valve seat is and almost nothing else on my list is.
- **It's been there since you bought it.** Carbon on an EGR seat is a slow, permanent condition, not a component that fails on a date.
- **EGR flow monitors run at cruise**, where the valve *does* open and *does* flow. So the self-test passes while the valve leaks at idle. No code, again.

## Ranked, with the rest

2. **Throttle body carbon / sticky electronic throttle.** Still live, still cheap, never cleaned in 131,000+ km. The wandering needle at a held pedal is a control-loop signature — on drive-by-wire your foot sets a request, not a plate position, so a plate that sticks makes the PCM hunt. Weakened slightly by the A/C test showing no change.
3. **Unmetered air — purge valve stuck open, PCV, intake gasket.** Textbook fit for the load pattern, but the years of no lean codes is a real argument against it. Not dismissed; demoted.
4. **Charge motion control plates stuck open**, if this manifold has them — I could not confirm the 3.7 uses CMCV, so treat that as unverified. Mechanically it would be a perfect match: lose tumble at low load, keep it normal at high load.
5. **Injector spray at idle-length pulse widths.** The clean didn't fix it, but it didn't clear it either.

## What would prove me wrong

- **Fuel trims strongly positive at idle** (say +15% or more) that fall toward zero at 2,500 rpm → it's an air leak, not EGR, and #3 becomes #1.
- **EGR reading fully closed at idle with a healthy flow test** → I'm wrong, drop to #2.
- **One cylinder standing out in the misfire counters** → my whole "global fault" reasoning collapses and it's that cylinder.

## The test that settles it

Two PIDs, while the truck is still in the workshop: **EGR actual position at warm idle** (must read fully closed — anything else is your answer) and **LTFT both banks at idle vs 2,500 rpm** (tells you leak-versus-dilution). If they'll do one more thing, have them physically pull the EGR valve and look at the seat — carbon holding it off its seat is visible, and it costs nothing but a gasket.

Worth saying plainly: everything bought so far — plugs, filter, injector clean, fluids, the O2 work — was aimed at faults that get *worse* under load. Your truck is perfect under load. None of it was ever going to fix this, and no more parts should go on it until those two numbers are read.

That's four more branches closed. Here's where the whole investigation now stands.

## Eliminated

| Ruled out | By what evidence |
|---|---|
| Fuel delivery — pump, filter, pressure | Pulls great under load; those fail under load first |
| Compression, valves, cam timing, phasers, chain | Same — all worsen with load, yours vanishes |
| Ignition — plugs, coils, boots | Plugs changed nothing, and ignition faults bite under load |
| Mounts, damper, flexplate, driveline, exhaust contact | Reproduces standing still in Park; D and R feel identical; nothing mechanical wanders a tach needle |
| O2 sensors as *root* cause | Identical cold, where the PCM runs open loop and ignores them |
| MAF / metering error | Factory airbox and duct, no oiled filter |
| Fuel quality or octane | Always 95, always the same station, symptom utterly constant |
| Aftermarket PCM tune | No tuner ever used, no evidence of prior engine work |
| Unlearned adaptive memory | Relearn done and 300 km driven since — trims will be mature and meaningful |
| Intake manifold gasket disturbed during the injector job | Manifold came off and went back on with *exactly* zero change |

That last one cuts both ways and is worth noticing: a manifold that was removed and refitted with no change to the symptom is mild evidence the fault isn't at the manifold face at all.

## What survives

1. **EGR not seating** — never touched in twelve years. My top call, and completely unexamined.
2. **Throttle body / electronic throttle carbon** — never cleaned in 131,000+ km.
3. **PCV valve, hose and elbow** — never inspected. Twelve years of Jeddah heat on rubber.
4. **EVAP purge valve stuck partly open** — never inspected either.
5. **Injector spray at idle-length pulse widths** — cleaned once on-car with no change, which doesn't clear it.

Every survivor is a low-load, high-vacuum charge or air-control fault. That's not a coincidence — it's the same conclusion the load curve gave, arrived at independently through the maintenance history. Two different routes to the same small set is the strongest thing I've got without scan data.

**One correction to my earlier reasoning:** I leaned on "no codes ever" fairly hard. The battery disconnect wiped stored and pending memory, so that argument is weaker than I made it sound. The MIL never illuminating is still valid evidence and I'd still bet on dilution over a leak — but read the **permanent** DTCs specifically. Those survive a battery disconnect and a scan-tool clear, and they're the one place a wiped history can't hide.

## For the workshop, while it's still there

1. Fuel trims, both banks — idle in P, idle in D, 2,500 rpm no load
2. **EGR actual position at warm idle** — must be fully closed
3. Permanent DTCs, not just stored and pending
4. Per-cylinder misfire counters — I predict all six roughly equal
5. Smoke test the intake, with purge and PCV isolated so a leaking valve can't hide as a system leak
6. Pull the EGR valve and look at the seat — a gasket and twenty minutes

Then clean the throttle body and run the relearn as housekeeping while things are open.

Two questions I didn't ask because they don't bear on this shake, but flag them to the workshop anyway: whether it has ever lost coolant with no visible leak — your engine has the internal timing-chain-driven water pump, and that failure mode puts coolant in the oil — and the condition of the valve cover gaskets.

All three came back clean, and none of them opens a new branch:

- **Thermostat is fine** — it reaches temperature and holds. So the PCM does exit warm-up enrichment, which means your "identical cold and hot" observation is real and not an artifact of an engine that never warms up. Closed-loop mixture faults stay ruled out.
- **5W-30 is slightly heavy** against Ford's 5W-20, and worth correcting at the next change — but it is not your cause. Sluggish phasers hurt *more* under load, and your truck is flawless under load. Housekeeping, not a fix.
- **Coolant steady** clears the internal water pump concern and rules out coolant intrusion into a cylinder.

## Where the maintenance audit leaves things

It produced no new leads — which is itself the finding. Everything that has been serviced is either done or irrelevant to this fault, and **every surviving suspect is something nobody has ever touched in twelve years:**

| Suspect | Ever inspected? |
|---|---|
| **EGR valve seat** — my top call | Never |
| Throttle body / electronic throttle | Never cleaned, 131,000+ km |

That's a tidy result. Three independent routes — the load curve, the maintenance history, and the elimination table — all converge on the same short list, and the list is entirely made of parts that have never been looked at.

## The sheet for the workshop

While it's still on the lift:

1. **EGR actual position at warm idle** — must read fully closed. Anything else is your answer.
2. **Fuel trims, both banks** — idle in P, idle in D, 2,500 rpm no load. Strongly positive at idle falling toward zero at 2,500 means an air leak instead; near-normal trims with a rough idle means dilution, and that's EGR.

4. **Per-cylinder misfire counters** — I predict all six roughly equal. One outlier and my reasoning is wrong.
5. **Smoke test the intake**, with purge and PCV isolated.
6. **Pull the EGR valve and look at the seat** — a gasket and twenty minutes, and carbon holding it open is visible to the eye.

Then throttle body clean plus idle relearn as housekeeping while things are open.

Don't let anyone fit another part before those numbers exist. Everything bought so far targeted faults that get worse under load, and your truck is perfect under load — which is why none of it worked.

Same caveat as before: forums are blocked in this container, so these are search summaries, not pages I opened.

## First, a correction — my top suspect doesn't exist on your engine

**The 3.7L Ti-VCT has no external EGR valve.** Ford uses the twin independent cam phasers to create *internal* EGR by controlling valve overlap — retarding the exhaust cam pulls exhaust back into the cylinder and does the EGR job, so the valve was deleted. Two independent sources say this, and RockAuto's catalog is consistent with it: the 2014 F-150 **3.5L EcoBoost** lists an EGR valve control solenoid, the **3.7L** does not.

So "pull the EGR valve and look at the seat" was wrong advice — there's nothing to pull. That also makes `CLAUDE.md` wrong a second time: it lists "EGR actual position at idle" as blocking scan data, and that PID likely doesn't exist on this truck.

The *mechanism* I described survives, though. Exhaust dilution at idle is still exactly what your symptom looks like — it just comes from the cam phasers, not a valve.

## The match I'd act on first

**EVAP purge valve stuck partly open.** Reported as *"a very common failure point on the 12th generation F-150 (2009–2014)"* — your exact generation. The described symptom set:

- stuck open = a **constant vacuum leak**, unmetered air and fuel vapour into the intake
- **rough idle where the engine shakes or sputters when stopped**
- worst at low speed and at a stop, can stall in severe cases
- **can happen without setting any code**
- part is **$30–70**

Rough when stopped, no code, twelve years untouched, your exact truck generation. That's the closest match to your profile I've found in this entire search.

**Two free tests, both from the search results:**
1. Engine running — unplug the purge valve's electrical connector. If you can still feel vacuum at the inlet port with your finger, it's stuck open.
2. Engine off — disconnect the EVAP line at the valve and apply a hand vacuum pump to the inlet. If it won't hold vacuum, it's bad.

## The transformed EGR suspect: VCT solenoid / phaser

Reported directly: *"rough idle that smooths under load aligns with typical VCT solenoid behavior"*, and *"if you search for stalling F-150 at idle you will come across a lot and most cases are VCT."* Root causes cited are oil-flow related — a clogged or faulty VCT solenoid starving the phaser, or a damaged solenoid screen letting debris through. The isolation test is clean: **swap the solenoid bank to bank** before condemning a phaser, and watch whether commanded cam position tracks or moves erratically.

**Two honest tensions with your case**, which is why this sits second and not first: VCT faults normally set P0010–P0024 and you've had none, and the classic description is "rough when cold, smooths when warm" — yours is identical cold and hot. Neither kills it, but neither supports it.

## Revised order

| # | Suspect | Why | Cost to test |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **EVAP purge valve stuck open** | Exact generation, exact symptom, codeless failure documented | Free, 2 minutes |
| 2 | **PCV valve, hose, elbow** | Never inspected, 12 years of Jeddah heat, same leak signature | Free, visual |
| 3 | **VCT solenoid / phaser** | Correct successor to the EGR mechanism | Bank swap, or read cam position PIDs |
| 4 | Vacuum leak elsewhere | Manifold refit changed nothing, but not excluded | Smoke test |

Injectors, throttle body, MAF, fuel, ignition, mechanical, mounts, thermostat and tune all stay eliminated.

**Do test 1 before you let anyone open anything.** It costs nothing, needs no tools, and it targets the single best-matching documented failure for your generation of truck.

===== [33] USER 2026-09-02T21:47:46.827Z =====
how to test each

===== [34] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T21:48:52.254Z =====
Ordered cheapest-first. Each one: what to do, what you should see, and what it means.

Safety, once: fuel vapour is involved in the first test — no ignition sources, no smoking. Watch the belt and fan whenever you're reaching into a running engine.

---

## 1. EVAP purge valve — the free one, do it first

The valve is normally **closed** when de-energized. Everything below tests that.

**Test A — the pinch test (30 seconds, no tools)**
1. Warm engine, idling in Park, A/C off.
2. Find the hose from the purge valve to the intake manifold.
3. Clamp or pinch it fully shut. Watch the tachometer and feel the shake for ~20 seconds.

- *Idle steadies, shake reduces, rpm may rise slightly* → **the valve was leaking. Found it.**
- *No change at all* → the valve is not passing. Move on.

**Test B — vacuum hold, engine off**
1. Engine off. Disconnect both hoses from the valve.
2. Hand vacuum pump on the canister-side (inlet) port.
3. Pull vacuum with the connector unplugged.

- *Holds steady* → valve seals, good.
- *Bleeds down* → **stuck open, replace.**

**Test C — feel for flow, engine running**
1. Idling, unplug the valve's electrical connector so the PCM cannot command it open.
2. Pull the canister-side hose off and put a finger over the port.

- *You feel vacuum pulling* → **it's passing with no command. Stuck open.**
- *Nothing* → sealed.

**Test D — with the scan tool**
Command purge duty to 0% at idle, then to 100%. Rpm and short-term trim should visibly react when commanded open, and be dead flat when commanded closed. No reaction either way, or reaction when commanded closed, condemns it.

Part is cheap. If any of A–C is positive, replace rather than clean.

---

## 2. PCV valve, hose and elbow

**Test A — flex it hot.** Engine warm and running, work the hose and both elbows by hand along their length. Heat-hardened rubber cracks open when flexed and closes up cold — this is why a cold visual inspection misses it. Look for glazing, crazing, or a chalky surface.

**Test B — pinch test.** Same as the purge valve: clamp the PCV hose at idle.
- *Rough idle smooths* → air was getting in there.
- *Idle rises slightly then settles* → normal.
- *No change at all* → suspicious in itself; that hose should be flowing.

**Test C — oil filler cap.** At idle, lift the oil filler cap slowly.
- *Idle changes a little, settles* → normal.
- *Idle drops hard or nearly stalls* → the system is drawing heavily; ventilation restricted.
- *Idle actually smooths out* → you have a crankcase pressure problem.

One thing to confirm before you buy a part: **whether your 3.7 has a serviceable PCV valve at all** — several Ford engines of this era integrate a fixed orifice into the valve cover with no replaceable valve. Check the parts catalogue against your VIN. I could not verify this either way.

---

## 3. Vacuum leak, anywhere else

**Test A — smoke test.** The definitive one, and your workshop has the machine.
1. Seal the intake at the throttle body or duct.
2. **Clamp off both the PCV and purge lines first** — otherwise a leaking valve shows up as "system leaks" and tells you nothing about *where*.
3. Introduce low-pressure smoke, then look along the manifold gaskets, throttle body base, every hose and fitting, the brake booster hose, and the dipstick tube.

Run it twice: once with those two lines clamped, once unclamped. The difference between the two runs isolates the valves from the manifold.

**Test B — vacuum gauge on a manifold port, warm idle.**
- *Steady 18–22 inHg* → healthy at your elevation.
- *Steady but low* → leak, or retarded timing.
- *Needle drifting slowly across a range* → mixture instability — corroborates the leak hypothesis.
- *Rapid regular flicker* → valve or guide problem.

**Test C — brake booster.** Clamp the booster hose at idle. If the idle changes, the booster or its check valve is leaking.

I'd avoid spraying flammable cleaner around a hot running engine to hunt leaks. Smoke does the same job with none of the risk.

---

## 4. VCT solenoid and phasers

**Test A — scan data, both banks at idle.** Watch commanded versus actual cam position.
- *Actual tracks commanded smoothly* → VCT is working.
- *Actual wanders, lags, or hunts* → solenoid or phaser.

**Test B — bank swap.** Swap the two VCT solenoids side to side.
- *The misbehaviour moves to the other bank* → **the solenoid.** Cheap.
- *It stays put* → phaser, chain, or oil supply.

**Test C — inspect the solenoid screens.** Pull them and look for debris or a holed screen. A damaged screen lets contamination into the phaser.

**Test D — oil pressure at hot idle.** Mechanical gauge, not the dash lamp. Reported requirement is **at least 25 psi at hot idle** for the phasers and tensioners to work — but I could not open the source document, so treat that number as unconfirmed and check it against the service manual.

**Test E — chain stretch.** The reported TSB method is **VCT_INT_ACT1 at idle: +6° or more means the chain is worn**, and with the cover off, **more than 5 teeth of tensioner extension** confirms it. Same caveat — reported, not verified by me.

---

## 5. The scan numbers, whatever else you do

**Fuel trims — LTFT and STFT, both banks, three conditions:** warm idle in P, idle in D, held 2,500 rpm no load.
- *High positive at idle, falling toward zero at 2,500* → **unmetered air leak.** Points at 1, 2 or 3.
- *High positive at both* → metering or fuel supply.
- *Near normal despite a rough idle* → **dilution, not mixture.** Points at 4.
- *Negative* → running rich; different diagnosis entirely.

**Permanent DTCs (Mode $0A)** — these survived your battery disconnect. Stored and pending were wiped; these weren't.

**Misfire counters, per cylinder** — I predict all six roughly equal. One clear outlier and my whole global-fault reasoning is wrong and it's that cylinder.

**Upstream HO2S switching** — at 2,500 rpm a healthy sensor sweeps roughly 0.1 to 0.9 V several times a second. Slow, lazy, or narrow swings mean the "cleaning" damaged them. Separate from your shake, but it's real and it's owed.

---

**Run them in that order.** Tests 1 and 2 are free, need nothing but your hands, and target the two components on this truck that have never been touched in twelve years. Don't pay for a smoke test until you've done them.

That changes my read, and it's worth saying directly: **you may not have a fault at all.**

Here's what your description actually is — a fine vibration you feel rather than hear, a small rpm movement, and an engine that sounds completely normal from under the hood. That is not what a diluted or misfiring engine sounds like. A charge dilution or vacuum leak bad enough to shake a cab makes an idle that is *audibly* uneven — lumpy, loping, a wavering exhaust note you can hear standing at the fender. You're telling me a trained ear at the hood can't tell there's anything wrong.

## The evidence that this is normal

Look at what your own history says:

- **It has never been smooth**, in your entire ownership. Not a change — a characteristic.
- **No code in years**, across every condition.
- **Pulls great, zero vibration under load.**
- **Idle rpm is normal**, 650–750, and stable enough that nothing stalls or nearly stalls.
- **Throttle body: properly removed, hand-cleaned. No change.**
- **Injectors: removed, cleaned, flow-tested. No change.**
- **Plugs, filter, all fluids. No change.**
- **Sounds normal from the hood.**

Six competent interventions, aimed at six different systems, every one of them producing exactly nothing. The simplest explanation for a fault that resists every repair is that there is no fault.

And the truck's specification fits. Yours is a **regular cab XL** — the base work truck, minimal sound deadening, no acoustic glass, a cab bolted close to the engine with little between you and it. The 3.7 is a **60° V6**, which is not inherently balanced the way an inline-six or a cross-plane V8 is; it has a rocking couple by design. A small idle vibration reaching the seat of a bare-bones regular cab is, plausibly, just what this truck is.

**Your D-versus-N result points the same way.** Every automatic is smoother in gear at a standstill — the converter loads and damps the engine. That's normal behaviour on every torque-converter vehicle ever built. It was never evidence of a fault; the repo built a whole theory on it.

## Settle it before spending anything more

**Test 1 — drive another one.** You're in a workshop. Find another 2014-ish F-150 regular cab with the 3.7, put your hand on the fender at idle, then sit in the cab. This is the single best test available to you and it costs nothing. A control sample answers in two minutes what six repairs haven't answered in years.

**Test 2 — quantify the rpm movement.** Don't trust the needle, log it. Watch the RPM PID at a steady warm idle in Park for 60 seconds:
- **±25 to 50 rpm of gentle wander** → normal closed-loop idle control. Every engine does this. Not a fault.
- **±100 rpm or more, or a visible rhythmic hunt** → a real fault, and my earlier list applies.

That threshold is the whole question. Your `obd_logger.py` already logs rpm — this is exactly what it's for.

**Test 3 — fuel trims.** LTFT within roughly ±10% at idle means the engine is metering correctly and there is no meaningful leak. Combined with misfire counters at or near zero, that is a clean bill of health for combustion, and you can stop.

## What I'd still do, because it's free

The purge valve pinch test and the PCV flex test from my last message. Two minutes, no parts, no tools. If either changes the idle you've found something real. If neither does, combined with normal trims and a control truck that feels the same — you're done, and the answer is that the truck is fine.

**Do not buy another part until tests 1 and 2 are done.** You have spent money on six repairs chasing a symptom nobody has yet established is abnormal. Establishing that costs nothing and it's the step that was skipped at the start.

If the numbers come back outside those thresholds, then it's a genuine fault, my ranked list stands, and I'll work it with you from there.

===== [37] USER 2026-09-02T21:54:28.933Z =====
why did you elemente phaser and smoke test

===== [38] ASSISTANT 2026-09-02T21:54:58.535Z =====
Neither is eliminated. One of them I got wrong, the other I only re-sequenced. Taking them separately:

## The phaser — I did eliminate it, and that was wrong

Early on I ruled out "cam timing, phasers, chain" on the grounds that they all worsen under load and your truck is perfect under load. That argument was too broad, and it collapsed two different failure modes into one:

- **A phaser stuck in a fixed wrong position** — that *does* cost power under load, and your truck pulling great genuinely rules it out.
- **A phaser or VCT solenoid that misbehaves specifically at idle** — that does the opposite. Oil pressure is at its lowest at idle, so phaser authority is weakest exactly there. Cam position wanders, rpm wanders with it, and the moment oil pressure comes up with rpm and load, it controls properly and goes smooth.

The second mode fits your load curve rather than contradicting it, and my elimination never applied to it. I reinstated it as **suspect #3** two messages ago when I found the 3.7 has no external EGR valve and does its exhaust recirculation through the phasers instead — but I didn't flag that I was reversing myself, and I should have.

Its two weaknesses are still real: VCT faults normally set P0010–P0024 and you've had no codes, and the usual description is rough-when-cold-smooths-when-warm, while yours is identical cold and hot. That's why it sits at 3 rather than 1, not eliminated.

## The smoke test — never eliminated, just sequenced later

It's still in the plan. I put it behind the free tests for two reasons:

**It costs shop time and the pinch tests don't.** The purge valve and PCV pinch tests target the same failure the smoke test would find, take two minutes, and need no equipment. Do the free version first.

**More importantly, a smoke test finds leaks that don't matter.** Any twelve-year-old engine will show *some* smoke somewhere. That tells you a leak exists, not whether it's doing anything to your idle. **Fuel trims tell you that.** If LTFT sits within ±10% at idle, no leak present is significant enough to explain a shake, and a smoke test then sends you chasing a finding that isn't your problem. If trims are strongly positive at idle and fall toward zero at 2,500 rpm, you have a leak that matters — and *then* the smoke test is the right tool to locate it.

So: trims decide whether to smoke test, not the other way round.

## Current order

1. Quantify the rpm movement, and feel a comparison truck — establishes whether there's a fault at all
2. Fuel trims, misfire counters, permanent DTCs
3. Purge valve and PCV pinch tests — free, do them alongside
4. **Smoke test** — if trims show a leak that matters
5. **VCT: cam position tracking at idle, bank-swap the solenoids** — if trims are normal but the rpm movement is genuinely out of range, since a dilution fault shows a rough idle *with* normal trims

Step 5 is exactly where the phaser lives, and it's the branch you land on if the trims come back clean but the instability is real.

نعم، أرسلها — الفكرة صحيحة وتتفق تمامًا مع أول ثلاثة احتمالات في القائمة. لكن فيها ثلاث نقاط تحتاج تعديل قبل ما ترسلها، وإلا ممكن تطلع نتيجة مضللة.

## التعديلات الثلاثة

**١. خط "الثلاجة" — على الأغلب ما فيه شيء تفصله.** موديل ٢٠١٤ يستخدم محركات كهربائية لأبواب التكييف، مو خراطيم هواء مثل السيارات القديمة. الفكرة هذي جاية من منتديات تتكلم عن سيارات أقدم. تأكد بصريًا، بس لا ترسل الميكانيكي يدور على شيء غالبًا غير موجود. **[يحتاج تأكيد من كتالوج السيارة]**

**٢. واحد واحد، مو كلهم مرة وحدة.** إذا فصل ثلاثة خطوط وسكّرها في نفس الوقت وتحسّن الوضع — ما راح يعرف أي واحد كان السبب، ولازم يعيد كل شيء من جديد. كل خط لحاله، مع مراقبة عداد الدوران قبل وبعد.

**٣. الأفضل يقرص الخرطوم بدل ما يفصله.** القرص (بكماشة خراطيم) أسرع، وما يحتاج سدادات، وما يخلق تسريب جديد. إذا فصل الخرطوم وسدّ المنفذ بسدادة غير محكمة، صار عنده تسريب أكبر من اللي يدوّر عليه، والنتيجة تطلع بالعكس.

## الرسالة جاهزة للإرسال

> السيارة: فورد F-150 موديل ٢٠١٤ — محرك 3.7 لتر Ti-VCT.
> العَرض: رجّة بسيطة تُحس في الكابينة على الرلنتي مع حركة بسيطة في عداد الدوران. المحرك ما يخنق ولا يطفي، وتحت الحمل ممتاز تمامًا بدون أي اهتزاز. ما طلع أي كود إطلاقًا.
>
> **المطلوب — اختبار تسريب هواء، خط خط، مع مراقبة العداد قبل وبعد كل خطوة:**
>
> ١. **بلف الكانستر (Purge Valve):** اقرص الخرطوم الرايح من البلف إلى المانيفولد والمحرك شغال على الرلنتي. لو ثبت الرلنتي أو قلّت الرجّة → البلف يسرّب. اختبار ثاني: افصل الكونكتر الكهربائي والمحرك شغال، وحسّ بإصبعك على منفذ البلف — إذا فيه شفط والبلف مو مأمور بالفتح، معناه مفتوح ومعلّق.
>
> ٢. **خرطوم الـ PCV:** حرّكه واثنِه **وهو حار والمحرك شغال** — المطاط المتشقق من الحرارة ينفتح وهو حار ويسكّر وهو بارد، فالفحص البارد ما يكشفه. بعدها اقرصه بشكل مؤقت وشوف الرلنتي.
>
> ٣. **خرطوم بوستر الفرامل:** اقرصه على الرلنتي. لو تغيّر الرلنتي → البوستر أو صمامه يسرّب. **احترس: السيارة واقفة فقط، لأن الفرامل تفقد التقوية.**
>
> ٤. **فحص الدخان:** فقط بعد قراءة نسب الوقود (Fuel Trims). ولو سويته، اقفل خط الكانستر وخط الـ PCV أولًا، وإلا أي بلف مسرّب راح يظهر كأنه تسريب في النظام كله وما يحدد المكان.
>
> **مطلوب كمان من جهاز الفحص:**
> - نسب الوقود LTFT للبنكين: رلنتي في P، ورلنتي في D، وعلى ٢٥٠٠ لفة بدون حمل
> - عدّادات الـ Misfire لكل سلندر
> - الأكواد الدائمة (Permanent / Mode $0A) — البطارية فُصلت سابقًا فمسحت المخزّنة والمعلّقة
> - وضع الكامات: المأمور مقابل الفعلي على الرلنتي للبنكين
>
> **ملاحظة مهمة:** المحرك هذا **ما فيه صمام EGR خارجي**. الـ Ti-VCT يسوي إعادة العادم داخليًا عن طريق تداخل الصمامات. لا تدوّر على صمام EGR ولا تطلب قراءة موقعه.

## نقطة أخيرة

قرص خرطوم الـ PCV راح يغيّر الرلنتي شوي حتى لو كل شيء سليم — لأنه مسار هواء نظامي. المهم هو **هل تقل الرجّة**، مو مجرد تغيّر الرقم. وخلّ القرص لثواني بس، لأن سد التهوية يرفع ضغط الكرتير.

وتبقى النصيحة الأساسية: الاختبارين المجانيين — مقارنة السيارة بسيارة ثانية نفس الموديل، وتسجيل حركة الـ RPM لمدة دقيقة — قبل ما تصرف على فحص دخان أو أي قطعة.

## The constraint, honestly

**This session can't reach your laptop.** I'm running in a cloud container — no USB, no serial port, no view of your hardware. So "connect me to the vehicle" doesn't work directly from here. Two ways it does work:

**Option A — you log, I analyse.** You run `src/obd_logger.py` on your own laptop at the truck, then send me the CSV. I do the analysis here. Works today, no setup beyond what's already in the repo.

**Option B — run Claude Code on your own laptop.** Then the agent has real serial access: it opens the adapter, runs the protocol live, sees each result, and decides the next test from what it just measured. That's the genuine "connected" version, and it's the one worth doing.

## What I'd actually do with live access

**Phase 0 — ask the truck what it supports.** Mode 01 PID `00`/`20`/`40` return bitmaps of every supported PID. That settles the EGR question empirically on *your* truck instead of from forum posts: if PIDs `2C`/`2D` come back unsupported, the no-external-EGR finding is confirmed on your VIN, not inferred. Takes three requests.

**Phase 1 — is it a fault?** 120 seconds of RPM at warm idle in Park, then compute mean, standard deviation, peak-to-peak, and — the part a scan tool screen can't do — **autocorrelation**. That distinguishes the two cases that look identical to the eye:

- **Random scatter of ±25–50 rpm** → normal closed-loop control. Not a fault.
- **A periodic hunt** — energy at a repeating interval → a real oscillation, and its period tells you what's driving it.

**Phase 2 — the load sweep.** Log trims, MAP, MAF, load and timing through: warm idle in P → idle in D on the brake → 2,500 rpm no load → back to idle. One continuous log, with the transitions visible.

**Phase 3 — the correlation, which is the real prize.** With rpm and short-term trim logged on the same timebase I can ask which one moves first:

- **STFT swings, then rpm follows** → fuel control is *causing* the instability. Look at metering, O2 sensors, leaks.
- **Rpm wanders while trims sit flat** → the engine is being disturbed and fuel control is just watching. That's **dilution or mechanical** — and it's exactly the signature that points at the phasers.

That single question is the one your six repairs never answered, and you cannot see it on a live-data screen. You need both channels timestamped together.

**Phase 4 — instrumented intervention.** Log continuously while you pinch the purge hose, then the PCV hose. The log shows the before/after objectively instead of "I think it felt smoother." Same for unplugging the purge connector.

**Phase 5 — supporting numbers.** MAP ripple at idle is a direct read on how evenly the cylinders are contributing. MAF at warm idle should land somewhere near 4–5 g/s for a 3.7 (rule of thumb, ~1 g/s per litre — not a Ford spec). Timing advance wandering at a steady idle is the PCM chasing something.

## What generic OBD-II cannot give you

Be clear-eyed about this — three things on my list are **not** reachable through standard Mode 01:

| Needed | Where it lives |
|---|---|
| Per-cylinder misfire counters | Mode `06` monitor data, or Ford enhanced PIDs |
| Permanent DTCs | Mode `0A` |
| **VCT commanded vs actual cam position** | **Ford enhanced only — needs FORScan or Ford IDS** |

The logger currently does Mode 01 only. For the VCT branch you need **FORScan on a Windows laptop** with an adapter that supports it — that's not something I can synthesise from generic PIDs.

## What I can build right now

The gap between "what the logger does" and "what the diagnosis needs" is real, and it's code I can write in this repo today:

1. **Mode `03`/`07`/`0A`** — stored, pending and permanent DTCs. Permanent codes are the only history that survived your battery disconnect, and right now nothing in the repo can read them.
2. **Mode `06`** — on-board monitor results, which is where misfire counts live on a generic tool.
3. **Supported-PID scan** — Phase 0 above.
4. **O2 sensor PIDs** — settles the "were they damaged" question.
5. **An analysis script** — feed it a CSV, get back the rpm statistics, the periodicity check against the ±50/±100 thresholds, the trims at each load point, and the rpm-versus-STFT correlation from Phase 3.

That last one is the piece that turns a log into an answer.

Want me to build these? I'd start with the DTC modes and the analysis script — those two alone would let you take one log at the truck and get a real verdict back, without needing FORScan first.

## The session, as I'd run it

120 seconds of RPM at warm idle in Park, sampled as fast as the adapter allows. Then I compute mean, σ, peak-to-peak, and run an autocorrelation to separate random control scatter from a periodic hunt.

**Minutes 20–30 — the load sweep.**
One continuous log through idle in P → idle in D on the brake → 2,500 rpm no load → back to idle. Logging rpm, MAP, MAF, both banks' trims, load, timing advance.

Then the analysis that a scan tool screen physically cannot show you: **rpm and short-term trim on one timebase, cross-correlated.** Does STFT move first and rpm follow — fuel control causing it? Or does rpm wander while trims sit flat — the engine being disturbed and fuel control merely watching?

That answers leak-versus-dilution directly, and it's the question six repairs never asked.

**Minutes 30–40 — instrumented intervention.**
Logging runs continuously while you pinch the purge hose, release, pinch the PCV hose, release, then unplug the purge connector. Each event timestamped. Instead of "I think it felt smoother," you get the actual rpm σ before and after each one, with a number attached.

**Minutes 40–50 — O2 sensors.**
Sweep the sensor PIDs at 2,500 rpm and measure the switching rate directly. Settles whether that "cleaning" damaged them — a separate question, but you're owed the answer.

## What's there

```
git clone <repo> && cd F-150-2014
python3 -m venv .venv

```

That last command needs no vehicle and should end with `all checks passed` — it validates the decoders, DTC decoding, the condition evaluator, the periodicity detector, every protocol's step graph, and the knowledge base schema.

**Commands:** `ports`, `survey`, `dtc`, `live`, `analyze`, `protocols`, `run`, `kb`, `selftest`.

**Three protocols**, written as data rather than code so you and Claude can edit them between runs: `triage` (five-minute opener), `idle-quality` (the full adaptive investigation), `o2-health`.

`run idle-quality` walks the diagnosis this repo has been building: survey what the truck supports → read permanent codes → 120 s idle baseline → **branch on whether a fault exists at all** → if it does, load sweep in D and at 2,500 rpm → branch on the trim comparison → then clamp tests on the purge, PCV and booster lines, each one measured before and after rather than judged by feel. `--dry-run` rehearses every prompt without an adapter so you know what to have in your hand.

## The two things it does that a scan tool screen can't

**Scatter or a hunt.** Autocorrelation on the rpm log. I found a real bug building this — a perfectly regular ±2 rpm ripple was being flagged as a hunt. Idle control is a closed loop, so it always oscillates; without an amplitude gate the detector calls every healthy engine faulty. Gated at 30 rpm peak-to-peak now, and the self-test checks all three cases: random scatter, a 60 rpm hunt, a 3 rpm ripple.

**Cause or effect.** Cross-correlating fuel trim against rpm. Trim leading means fuel control is driving the instability; rpm leading means something else disturbs the engine and fuel control is only reacting. On a synthetic log with a known 0.6 s lag injected, it recovered the lag and the direction correctly.

## Where I did not claim parity

`DID_REGISTRY` ships **empty**. Ford doesn't publish these identifiers, and a wrong Mode 22 address doesn't fail — it returns a plausible number, which then condemns a good part. So cam position and Ford's misfire counters still need FORScan, and `docs/ENHANCED-PIDS.md` says exactly how to verify a DID against FORScan and promote it to `verified` rather than guessing.

Mode 06 values stay raw against raw limits — enough for pass/fail and margin. A guessed scale factor produces a confident wrong number.

## The knowledge base

12 entries, every one carrying `source`, `confidence`, and a `verified` flag that means *someone opened the source*. **None currently qualify** — this container blocks forums, TSBs and parts catalogues, so everything is search summaries and it's labelled that way. `kb validate` rejects any entry with no provenance or no test.

`load_signature` is the field that earns its keep: for a fault that never sets a code, mechanism is the only evidence, and `by_signature()` selects candidates on that rather than on a code that never appeared.

**This is the part local unblocks.** Your laptop can reach f150forum, ford-trucks, NHTSA and RockAuto — all blocked here. First useful local session: have Claude open the real sources and upgrade those 12 entries to `verified: true`, then add the ones we never got to read.

===== [69] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T14:55:11.359Z =====
Pushed. Both corrections were right and they made every clamp-based test I'd written unperformable.

## The new test order, and why it's this order

Every isolation is now: **engine off → disconnect → plug the manifold port → restart → settle 30 s → measure.**

**1. EVAP purge. 2. Brake booster. 3. PCV.**

Purge and booster first because on a healthy engine, sealing either port should change *nothing at all* — so any change is signal. PCV last because it's a metered air path in normal operation: blocking it shifts the idle even when nothing is wrong. The protocol now says explicitly to read whether the **instability** drops, not whether the rpm number moves.

Your restart requirement forced a change that improved the experiment. Since every test involves a start, the comparison baseline is now taken the same way — started, settled 30 s, measured. I'd been comparing against a long-settled idle, which would have shown a difference that meant nothing. All four measurements are now under identical conditions.

Added a restore step that checks no plug was left in a port — otherwise you've created the leak you spent the afternoon hunting.

## The Mustang angle was a good call

Same Cyclone engine, and that community is much larger. It's now a method note in the base recording what transfers — phasers, chain, water pump, PCV, purge valve, fuel trim behaviour — and what doesn't: mounts, driveline, exhaust, cab, NVH, installation and routing. **The engine is shared; the installation is not.**

Three things it turned up:

**Ford's own position on 3.7 vibration.** Mustang 3.7 owners raised a vibration complaint big enough to reach a public petition, and **Ford's stated position is that it's normal operation** — one owner reportedly told it's normal "because of the twin cam setup." That's the same engine as yours, and it bears directly on whether you have a fault at all.

Honest limit, and it's in the entry: the Mustang accounts describe **2200–2800 rpm and vibration through the shifter**, not an idle vibration. So it may be a different symptom entirely. The valuable part is Ford's position that a 3.7 vibration can be normal for this engine — but the check field says establish that before leaning on it.

**Purge valve.** Mustang sources say the common failure mode of Ford's purge valve is stuck *open* — my top suspect's exact mechanism. And a 2014 Mustang V6 case presented as **P2196/P2198, oxygen sensor signal biased rich** — not EVAP codes. Worth knowing: a leaking purge valve may not announce itself where you'd look.

**Your PCV question is largely answered.** On the Mustang 3.7 the PCV valve is on the **passenger-side valve cover, roughly halfway toward the front**, and it's removable — pull it and shake it, no rattle means clogged. That test is now in the protocol. Location still needs confirming on the truck, since the installation differs.

===== [70] USER 2026-09-04T15:11:37.957Z =====
check how can you connect your self with forscan when both of you running from same machine

===== [71] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T15:11:58.779Z =====
Good question, and the answer starts with a hard constraint. Let me verify the specifics rather than assert them:

===== [72] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T15:12:43.290Z =====
Verified enough to answer properly — and one of my earlier hopes turns out to be wrong.

## The hard constraint first

**You cannot both hold the adapter.** A serial port is exclusive: one process opens it, everyone else gets "access denied." FORScan and my tool alternate — one closes, the other opens. There's no sharing a single adapter, and no amount of software fixes that.

Two adapters on a Y-splitter is physically possible since CAN is a multi-drop bus, but two testers polling at once collide and get each other's responses. Don't.

So the integration has to be at the **file level**, not the port level. Let me build that.

===== [73] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T15:15:40.159Z =====
Built and pushed. The answer to your question is: **not through the port — through the filesystem.**

## Why not the port

A serial port is opened by one process at a time. Whoever gets there first holds it, everyone else is refused. That's the operating system, not the software, so no amount of cleverness fixes it. FORScan and `f150diag` take turns: one disconnects, the other connects.

## The division of labour

| Tool | Does |
|---|---|
| **FORScan** | Ford enhanced parameters — cam position desired vs actual above all |
| **f150diag** | Generic OBD, adaptive protocols, guided procedures, statistics, causality, knowledge base |

**New command:**

```
f150diag forscan <export>.csv
```

Run the protocol with my tool, disconnect, open FORScan, log VCT desired and actual for both banks plus rpm, save as **CSV** (not `.fsl` — that's FORScan's replay format), then feed it back. It runs the same analysis as a native log and adds cam tracking: worst tracking error, and whether actual position oscillates while the command sits still.

Tested on a synthetic export with a 9° cam oscillation injected — it caught it and gave the bank-swap next step.

## A correction to what I told you earlier

I'd said the plan was to extract the enhanced PIDs from FORScan's config files and populate `DID_REGISTRY`. **That doesn't work.** Reportedly custom PID definitions can't be added to FORScan's database at all, and vehicle profiles are stored coded rather than as readable definitions.

So importing an export gives us FORScan's **measurements, never its addresses**. `DID_REGISTRY` stays empty.

The documented route to the addresses is different and it does work: **capture the traffic FORScan sends to the adapter while it displays a parameter, and read the Mode 22 requests directly** — serial port monitor for a USB adapter, Wireshark for a network one. Vary the engine condition, watch which identifier's response tracks the value on screen. That produces a genuinely verified entry rather than a guess, and it's written up in `docs/FORSCAN.md`.

## One thing to check on your first real import

The column mapping is marked `[VERIFY]` throughout. It uses the conventional Ford mnemonics — `LONGFT1`, `VCT_INT_ACT1` and so on — but **none is confirmed against a real export from your truck.** A wrong mapping is worse than a missing one, because it relabels real data with the wrong name.

Every import prints exactly what it mapped and what it didn't, and unmapped columns are carried through under their original names rather than dropped. Read that report on the first import before trusting anything downstream of it, and tell me what FORScan actually calls its columns — I'll fix the table.

===== [80] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T15:41:21.446Z =====
Done. FORScan is now a step inside my diagnostic graph, not a separate errand.

## What I checked first

**FORScan has no command line, no API, no scripting interface** — nothing public. And `.fsl` is closed proprietary binary. So it can't be called as a library. But it can be *orchestrated*, and that's the difference between it being your tool and it being mine.

## The handoff step

```yaml
- id: vct_handoff
  type: handoff
  request: vct
  seconds: 90
  next: vct_verdict
```

When that runs, I:

1. **Close and release the adapter** — session stays live, only the port is given up
2. **Launch FORScan**
3. Print the exact parameter list to add and what to record
4. **Watch for the export** across the FORScan data folder, Documents, Downloads and Desktop — waiting for the file size to settle, because a file appears in a directory before it's finished being written
5. Import it, compute cam tracking, put the metrics into the protocol context
6. **Reconnect the adapter** and carry on branching

Step 4 is the whole point. "Export it and tell me the filename" puts you in the middle of every handoff. Now you record, save as CSV, and I pick it up on my own.

Branches then decide on FORScan's data like any other measurement:

```yaml
- when: vct_worst_error > 5 or vct_actual_periodic
  finding: vct-solenoid-phaser
```

## Where it fires

**`vct-check`** runs it standalone. **`idle-quality`** now offers it automatically when it finds an unstable idle with normal fuel trims — exactly your case if the trims come back clean, since that's where fuel control is excluded and cam timing is the only dilution path left on an engine with no external EGR. Both route a positive result into the bank-to-bank solenoid swap.

`f150diag run` also refuses to start if FORScan is already holding the port, rather than failing with a permission error halfway through a measurement.

## A bug I only found by running it

`forscan-status` reported **`running: True`** on this container. The Linux process check was matching **my own command line** — `f150diag forscan-status` contains the word "forscan." Every time I asked the question, I answered yes.

That would have blocked every session on a Linux laptop. Fixed to exclude this process, its parent, and anything whose command line is f150diag, with a regression check in the self-test. Worth noting I'd have shipped it if I'd only read the code instead of running the command.

## One thing to check on your first real handoff

The parameter names I ask FORScan for — `VCT_INT_ACT1`, `VCT_INT_DES1` and so on — are the conventional Ford mnemonics, **not confirmed against your truck's FORScan profile**. If FORScan calls them something else, the import will report those columns as unmapped rather than silently mislabelling them. Send me the mapping report from the first run and I'll correct the table.

===== [83] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T15:43:18.921Z =====
Yes — three, and two of them cost nothing. Same caveat as always: search summaries, pages not opened.

## 1. PCV valve shake test — 5 minutes, do this first

The Mustang 3.7 sources give the location and the test: **passenger-side valve cover, roughly halfway toward the front of the engine.** Pull the valve, shake it. **No rattle means it's clogged.**

Why this is the best fast check you have:

- Mustang 3.7 sources name **PCV valve clogging and vacuum leaks** as a known rough-idle cause *on this engine* — not on Fords generally
- It's never been inspected on your truck in twelve years of Jeddah heat
- It needs no scan tool, no engine running, no plugs or caps
- It's a few riyal if it's bad

Verify the location on the truck — the engine is shared with the Mustang, the installation isn't.

## 2. Purge valve vacuum-hold test — 5 minutes, engine off

Mustang sources state plainly that **the common failure mode of Ford's purge valve is stuck open.** That's my top suspect's exact mechanism, from the same-engine community.

Engine off, connector unplugged, hand vacuum pump on the inlet port. **It must hold vacuum.** If it bleeds down, it's stuck open and it's your fault — a permanent unmetered leak that's worst at idle and gone under load, which is your symptom exactly.

No stalling risk, no running engine, no plugging ports. This is the fastest definitive test in the whole diagnosis.

## 3. Ask Ford whether a newer PCM calibration exists for your VIN — free

Ford has issued PCM updates specifically for idle quality on other engines, and a calibration flash is a fix with no parts. Worth one phone call.

You can read what's currently loaded yourself:

```
f150diag --port /dev/ttyUSB0 survey
```

That prints the calibration IDs from service 09. Give those and the VIN to a Ford dealer and ask whether a later calibration is available. If yes, that's potentially a flash-and-done.

## The one document I'd most want opened

A search result surfaced an NHTSA bulletin titled **"Vibration/Rough Idle In DRIVE Or REVERSE, Lack Of…"** — `MC-10184634-0001`. That title matches your symptom pattern almost word for word.

**I could not open it** — NHTSA is blocked here — so I don't know which vehicles or engines it covers, and it may be irrelevant. But it's the single highest-value thing to fetch when you're local:

`https://static.nhtsa.gov/odi/tsbs/2020/MC-10184634-0001.pdf`

## Two things I'd skip, and why

**The oil-change report.** A 3.7 owner reported rough idle cured by getting factory blended oil out at the first change. Yours is **identical cold and hot**; theirs was rough-when-cold. Different symptom. Your 5W-30 against 5W-20 spec is still worth correcting at the next change, but it isn't this.

**Seafoam / carbon cleaning.** One owner suggested it, claiming the 3.7 is prone to rough idle from a dirty engine. Your 3.7 is **port injected**, not direct injected, so intake valve carbon is far less of an issue than on DI engines — and your injectors were flow-tested and your throttle body properly cleaned. Low odds.

## Honest expectation

Six competent repairs have already failed, so I wouldn't bet heavily on any quick win. But checks 1 and 2 target **the two components on this truck that have never been touched**, they take ten minutes together, and they need no tools you don't have.

If both come back clean, that's not a wasted morning — it's two of my top three suspects eliminated with evidence, and it moves the diagnosis straight to the rpm log and the trims.

Want me to add these as a `quick-wins` protocol so the tool walks you through them before the long one?

===== [101] USER 2026-09-04T19:26:43.399Z =====
[IMAGE]

===== [102] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T19:27:59.045Z =====
Real data — and it changes something the repo asserts. Let me read it carefully.

## What you've got

| Module | Code | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| OBD-II + **Engine control unit** | **U0422** | Invalid data received from body control module |
| OCS | **U0140** | No communication with BCM |
| RCM | **B11D8(14)** | Restraints event notification |

**All four are "Archive (inactive)".** They're history, not live faults.

## The headline: still no powertrain codes

Not one P-code. No misfire, no fuel trim, no VCT, no lean codes. The PCM's own monitors have nothing to say about how the engine runs — and that's the second independent line of evidence pointing the same way as the load curve.

But the repo's claim of **"no code in years" is now wrong** and I'll correct it. What's true is narrower: *no powertrain driveability codes*. There are network and restraint codes, and I shouldn't have let the broader statement stand unqualified.

## The most likely explanation for all of them

Three modules complaining about the BCM — invalid data, no communication — plus a restraint entry, **all inactive**. That's the classic signature of a **voltage event**, not four separate faults. Modules drop off the bus when voltage sags, and each one logs what it saw.

You disconnected the battery. That's in the repo. And U0422 reads *"Test failed since last DTC clear"* — so a clear happened, and this logged after it. The timeline fits.

## Does any of it explain the shake?

Almost certainly not. These are body-network and restraint codes, they're inactive, and your vibration has been constant since purchase — long before that battery disconnect. A U-code that isn't currently present can't produce a symptom that's present every second.

## But B11D8 deserves attention on its own

"Restraints event notification" in the RCM, alongside an OCS module that lost communication, is not something to file away. Two things I'd want to know:

1. **Is the airbag warning light on?** That's a thirty-second check and it changes the urgency completely.
2. Your history report claimed **no accident history**. A restraints event entry doesn't prove otherwise — a voltage drop can log one — but it's worth knowing which it is, given that same report also got your fuel type and drivetrain wrong.

I can't verify Ford's exact definition of `B11D8` or the `(14)` sub-code from here — the network blocks the sources. Flagging it as needing lookup rather than guessing.

## What I'd do

**Record these first** (your screenshot does that), then **clear them and drive normally for a week.** Whatever comes back is real; whatever doesn't was the voltage event. Normally I'd resist clearing — but there are no P-codes here, so there's no freeze frame or misfire history to lose, and return-after-clear is the only way to separate live from historical.

## The number that matters: short-term fuel trim

**STFT bank 1: 0.78% and 3.13%. Bank 2: 0%.**

That's the one to look at, not long-term. An unmetered air leak makes the mixture lean **the instant it exists** — short-term trim corrects positive immediately, before long-term learns anything. Yours sits between 0 and 3%.

**That eliminates the entire leak family by measurement.** Purge valve, PCV, brake booster, intake gaskets — the whole top of my suspect list, gone. Not by reasoning this time. By a number.

**Misfire monitor: available and COMPLETED, DTC count 0.** It ran and it passed. There is no misfire.

Everything else agrees: lambda 0.99, AFR 14.52, knock retard 0°, timing 11.5°, cam advance at its rest position, throttle tracking its target within 0.27°, catalyst temps identical across banks, ECT 94°C, charging 14V.

**That's a third independent line of evidence that there's no engine fault** — after the load curve and the absence of powertrain codes.

## The caveat, because it would be easy to oversell this

Only **101 km and 3 warm-ups since codes were cleared**, and the Fuel System monitor reads *"not completed."* LTFT of exactly 0.0% on both banks is more likely **not yet re-learned** than learned-and-perfect. Re-read it after a few hundred kilometres.

The short-term reading doesn't depend on learning, so it stands on its own. But don't let anyone quote the LTFT figure as proof yet.

## Your data broke one of my tests

**Commanded evaporative purge: 41% at warm idle.**

My idle-quality protocol said sealing the purge port *"should change nothing at all on a healthy system."* That's wrong. The PCM is actively commanding purge at idle on this truck — flow into the manifold there is normal and designed. Sealing that port changes a healthy idle too.

As written, the test would have condemned a good valve. Fixed: it now logs commanded purge alongside the result and distinguishes a valve flowing when it *isn't* commanded — a real fault — from flow proportional to a high commanded value, which is just the system working.

## Three anomalies I'm not glossing over

**Ethanol fuel percent: 16.08%.** Your truck is flex-fuel, and on a 2014 Ford the ethanol content is **inferred, not sensed**. Saudi pump fuel is normally E0. A PCM that believes it has E16 calculates base fuelling richer than needed — closed loop corrects that, open loop (cold start, WOT) does not. And the inference leans on the O2 sensors, which were "cleaned" by an unknown method. Verify against a second tool before acting, but it's worth watching.

**MAF 2.93 g/s at 661 rpm** — low-normal for a 3.7. Not alarming alone.

**Right front tyre 211.7 kPa** against a 241 kPa door label — about 4 psi low. Nothing to do with the idle, but real.

**The real body of the trace sits roughly 638–668** — a band about **30 rpm wide**. That's well inside normal closed-loop idle control.

## The structure, and why it isn't a hunt

There *is* a repeating pattern: rpm climbs to ~670–700, falls back to ~635, climbs again. Period roughly **four to five minutes**.

That's not an idle control hunt. A hunting idle oscillates over **seconds** — you see the needle breathe and you feel it. A four-minute cycle is the PCM responding to slowly changing loads: **the cooling fan cycling** at 37 °C ambient, **purge cycling** (commanded at 41 %, as your earlier scan showed), alternator load. All normal idle management doing its job.

## The finding that matters most

**This measurement cannot see your vibration, and neither can any OBD log.**

At 660 rpm a V6 fires three times per revolution — that's **33 Hz**. That's the frequency of what you feel in the seat.

Your graphs span 10–15 minutes with a few hundred points: roughly **one sample every 2–4 seconds**, about 0.3 Hz. The vibration is four orders of magnitude faster than this data can resolve. What the tachometer shows and what your body feels are two completely different phenomena, and I conflated them earlier by treating "visible tacho movement" and "felt shake" as one symptom.

I also need to correct my own threshold: I set ±25–50 rpm for a **120-second** window. Comparing a 10-minute span against it isn't like-for-like — slow load drift accumulates over ten minutes and inflates the number. On the right timescale, your idle is normal.

Electronically, it's finished. Every measurable parameter is healthy:

- No misfire — monitor completed and passed
- Short-term trims 0–3% — no leak, no mixture error
- Lambda 0.99, knock retard 0, cam at rest position, throttle tracking within 0.27°
- **Idle speed control working normally**

Four independent lines of evidence now say the same thing: **there is no engine fault to find.**

## The one test left, and it isn't OBD

Use your phone as a vibration analyser. Any accelerometer/spectrum app, phone flat on the seat or the console, engine at warm idle. Read the **dominant frequency**:

- **~33 Hz** → that's the firing pulse of a 60° V6. Normal combustion, felt through a bare regular-cab floor. Nothing to fix.
- **~11 Hz** → first-order rotational imbalance. Damper, pulley, flexplate. Something to chase.

That single number distinguishes "this truck vibrates because of what it is" from "something is out of balance" — and it measures the actual complaint instead of a proxy for it.

===== [117] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T19:50:53.900Z =====
That reverses my conclusion, and I need to say so plainly.

**A 15-second screen width means the pattern repeats every ~3 seconds.** Counting peaks across your traces: 4–6 per screen, so a period of roughly 2.5–4 seconds. And you can see the needle breathe, which independently confirms it's on a timescale of seconds.

I read the axis as wall-clock and concluded "idle control is working correctly." That was wrong, and it was the fourth of my four "no fault" pillars. It's gone.

## What oscillates at 3 seconds

That period is characteristic of the **closed-loop fuel control cycle**. The loop's speed is set by how fast the upstream O2 sensors switch. A slow sensor lengthens the loop and increases its overshoot — the engine goes rich, the sensor reports late, the correction overshoots lean, and round again. Every cycle moves the rpm slightly.

**Your O2 sensors were "cleaned" by an unknown method.** I've been treating that as a separate housekeeping item all along. It just became the leading suspect.

Other candidates at that period: purge cycling (commanded 41% at idle, measured) and idle-air control instability.

## The test that settles it costs nothing

**Watch the needle on a cold start, before the engine warms up.**

A cold engine runs **open loop** — the PCM ignores the O2 sensors entirely and fuels from a table. So:

- **Breathing absent when cold, appears once warm** → it's the closed-loop fuel control, and the O2 sensors are the cause
- **Breathing present cold and warm alike** → not the fuel loop; look at purge and idle air

You've told me the *shake* feels identical cold and hot. But nobody has ever asked whether the **needle breathing** is identical cold and hot — those are different observations and I conflated them.

Second free test, warm: **unplug the purge valve's electrical connector** and watch the needle for a minute. De-energised, the valve closes. No hose disconnection, no stall risk. If the breathing stops, it's purge.

Recording the correction.
