This is exactly what I needed. Both traces on one timebase, and it kills my leading suspect.

## Fuel control is not causing the hunt

Look at the STFT trace. It's **quantized in 0.78% steps** — that's one LSB of the PID's resolution — and it never leaves the band between **−1.56% and +1.56%**. Your session averages across the eight captures: −0.92, −0.44, −0.17, −0.39, −0.34, −0.25, −0.61, −0.47.

**That's essentially zero correction.** The mixture is right, continuously.

If the closed-loop fuel loop were hunting — the mechanism I proposed last message, driven by slow O2 sensors — STFT would be swinging ±10–20% in rhythm with the rpm. It isn't. It's toggling between adjacent resolution steps, which is just the loop dithering at its floor.

**The O2 sensor hypothesis is dead.** Those sensors are producing corrections far too small to move the idle 40 rpm. They may still be worth replacing on their own merits, but they are not causing this.

## What that leaves

Your rpm is oscillating ~40 rpm at a 3.5–4 second period **while the mixture stays correct**. So this is a **torque oscillation with correct fuelling** — the air or the spark is moving, not the fuel.

Three candidates:

1. **Idle air control** — the electronic throttle hunting its target. Air changes, rpm follows, and fuel tracks it proportionally through the MAF, so trim never has to correct. This fits the data best.
2. **Purge cycling** — purge adds air *and* fuel vapour together. Notably, **every one of your STFT averages is slightly negative**, meaning the PCM is trimming marginally rich. That's what a small vapour contribution looks like. Weak evidence, but it points the same way as the 41% commanded purge.
3. **Spark timing** — the PCM uses advance for fast idle correction.

**B — `Engine RPM` + `Commanded evaporative purge`**
If purge steps up and down on a 3.5–4 second rhythm matching the rpm, that's your answer.

**New — `Engine RPM` + `Throttle Position Actually`**
This is the one I'd now bet on. If the throttle angle is oscillating in rhythm, the idle air control loop is hunting and everything else follows from it.

Now I see what you're looking at — those tall vertical lines in the purge graph. They look dramatic, but check the **y-axis numbers**:

**Top of that axis is 40.78. Bottom is 40.40.** The entire graph covers a range of **0.38%**.

So those full-height spikes are the value flicking between **40.39 and 40.78** — and those two numbers are one step apart. The PID has a resolution of 0.392% (255 steps over 100%), so 40.39 and 40.78 are **adjacent values with nothing in between**. The commanded purge is sitting between them and the reported byte rounds one way, then the other.

It's rounding noise on a flat signal. There's no 47 anywhere — the Max readout under each graph says **40.78** on all three.

## The trap here, and I fell into it myself

**The app auto-scales the y-axis to whatever range the data occupies.** When a value is nearly constant, the axis zooms right in and normal rounding flicker fills the whole screen height.

That's exactly what made your STFT graph look like a violent square wave earlier — its axis only spanned ±1.5%. And it's why I read those slightly-negative trim averages as a possible vapour signature last message, which I then had to withdraw. Same mistake, twice, from the same cause.

Compare the rpm graph: axis **618 to 703**, an 85 rpm range, and the trace genuinely fills a third of it. That's a real oscillation. The purge trace looks bigger on screen and is 200 times smaller in reality.

**Rule for reading these: check the axis range before judging the amplitude.**

===== [136] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T20:17:03.923Z =====
You're right, and there's proof in your own screenshots: **Min 617 / Max 703 is identical on every single one**, and Avg only creeps 652.75 → 652.66. Those are session-cumulative figures, not the visible window. I've been quoting numbers that describe an hour of idling as though they described 15 seconds. Dropping them.

## Reading the traces themselves

Looking at the waveform rather than the summary:

**There are two periodicities superimposed, not one.**

- A **fast ripple, roughly 1–1.5 seconds**, amplitude around 8–12 rpm. Fine, continuous, present through every window.
- A **slower envelope, roughly 4–6 seconds**, amplitude around 20–25 rpm, which the fast ripple rides on top of.

**The shape is asymmetric.** Rises are ragged and stepped; falls are steeper and cleaner. That asymmetry matters — a symmetric sine would suggest a simple resonance, while a fast-fall/slow-rise pattern is what a control loop looks like when it corrects harder in one direction than the other.

Sample rate is roughly 5–8 per second (ping 55–92 ms, two PIDs), so the 1–1.5 s ripple is genuinely resolved. Anything faster than about 0.3 seconds is invisible.

**What that combination suggests:** a control loop running a small continuous limit cycle, plus a slower disturbance it's chasing. Not one mechanism — two.

## What next

The OBD side is nearly exhausted. One trace left, then better instruments.

**1. `Engine RPM` + `Throttle Position Actually`** — still not taken, still the discriminator. Throttle moving in rhythm means the PCM is generating the ripple. Throttle steady means something else is.

**2. Vacuum gauge on a manifold port.** This is the instrument I should have pushed harder. It reads manifold pressure in real time at a bandwidth no scan tool approaches — it responds to individual combustion events. At warm idle:

- Steady 18–22 inHg → healthy
- Needle drifting slowly across a range → mixture or the same slow disturbance
- Rapid regular flutter → valve or sealing
- Sharp periodic drop → one cylinder down

You're in a workshop with the truck warm. This takes five minutes and sees things the ELM327 physically cannot.

**3. Cylinder balance, by injector.** I should have proposed this several messages ago. Warm idle, unplug **one injector connector at a time**, note the rpm drop, plug it back. Six readings.

- Equal drops across all six → cylinders contributing evenly, and combustion is not your vibration
- One cylinder dropping noticeably less → that's the cylinder

Use the injectors, not the coils — killing a coil dumps raw fuel into the catalyst. Keep each disconnect to a few seconds. It'll set misfire codes; clear them after.

This directly tests combustion evenness, which is what a felt vibration would come from, and nothing in the OBD data has tested it — the misfire monitor passing only means no cylinder is bad enough to trip a threshold.

**4. Cold start, watch the needle.** Still outstanding, still free, still the cleanest open/closed-loop test.

If I had to pick one for right now: **the vacuum gauge.** It's the only instrument you have that can see faster than the hunt.

## What the trace shows

That's a governor doing exactly what it should: retard spark to kill torque when speed is high, advance it to add torque when speed is low.

## What that means

**The PCM is controlling idle speed with spark, not air.** That's why the throttle sat motionless — it isn't the actuator being used. Air is held constant; torque is trimmed with ignition timing, which acts far faster.

So the earlier discriminator needs restating: throttle static didn't mean "the PCM is passive." It meant "the PCM is using the other lever."

## The argument that settles the direction

Here's what I think is decisive, and it isn't from the graphs — it's arithmetic.

**Nothing in your engine cycles at 3.5–4 seconds.**

| Process | Frequency at 650 rpm |
|---|---|
| Firing events | ~33 Hz |
| Crankshaft rotation | ~11 Hz |
| Camshaft rotation | ~5.5 Hz |
| **Your oscillation** | **~0.28 Hz** |

A mechanical or combustion disturbance has to come from something that *moves*. Nothing in the engine moves at a quarter of a hertz. That period is a **control timescale** — it's the response time of a feedback loop with lag in it.

**This is the idle governor limit-cycling.** Not a mechanical fault.

## Honest limit

I can't determine lead versus lag by eye from screenshots. Spark and rpm are locked in a closed loop, and separating cause from effect needs the two cross-correlated on a synchronised log — which is precisely what the tool in your repo was built to do, and which this app can't give me.

## And a likely contributor

Your adaptives are **young**: 101 km and 3 warm-ups since codes were cleared, with LTFT sitting at exactly 0.0% on both banks — almost certainly un-relearned rather than perfect. The idle adaptive tables live in the same keep-alive memory that the battery disconnect wiped.

**A governor working from un-relearned adaptives hunts more than one that has settled.**

## What I'd do now

**1. Drive it.** Several hundred kilometres of normal use, then re-measure rpm and LTFT. This is free, it's owed regardless, and it's the single change most likely to move the numbers.

**2. Then re-measure and compare.** If the oscillation tightens, it was adaptation. If it's identical, it's calibration or something else.

**3. The comparison truck** — still the only way to know whether ±20 rpm on this engine is abnormal at all. A 3.5° spark swing at idle is genuinely modest; most PCMs modulate more than that.

**4. And separately: the felt vibration is still unexplained.** 0.28 Hz cannot be what you feel at 33 Hz. The phone accelerometer test is still the only thing that addresses your actual complaint.

===== [143] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T20:31:11.883Z =====
Good — that changes something and I need to correct myself twice.

## First: the adaptives hypothesis is dead

You did the relearn, drove 300 km, no change. Drop it. I'll withdraw it from the repo.

## Second, and more importantly: I over-claimed

I said "nothing in the engine moves at 0.28 Hz, therefore it's a control loop." That's true for **rotating** parts — crank, cam, firing — but it is **not** true for things that are *actuated* or that can *flutter*. Those can absolutely oscillate on a seconds timescale.

So the mechanical door isn't closed, and I shouldn't have closed it.

## Do you need more screenshots? Yes — one, and it's a real gap

**`Engine RPM` + `Variable camshaft actual advance #1`**

You have this PID. I never asked for it against rpm and I should have.

The cam phasers are a **PCM-actuated torque modulator** that operates on exactly this timescale. On this engine, valve overlap *is* your EGR — internal exhaust dilution — so a phaser oscillating at idle changes cylinder filling and therefore torque, at a few seconds per cycle. It's the one actuator left that could be driving the rpm rather than following it.

If cam advance is flat, that's genuinely eliminated. If it's swinging in rhythm, you have your answer.

## Is it wiring? It could be, and here's how to test it

Wiring is a real candidate I haven't pushed on. A **noisy crank position signal** makes the PCM *believe* the rpm is moving, and the governor then modulates spark for a disturbance that isn't there — which would look exactly like what we're seeing. Twelve years of Jeddah heat on connectors makes it plausible, and it sets no code if the signal stays within tolerance.

**Four tests, DMM only, about 20 minutes:**

**1. Alternator ripple.** DMM on **AC volts**, probes across the battery terminals, engine idling.
- **Under 0.1 V AC** → charging system clean
- **Above that** → a failed diode in the alternator, putting AC ripple onto the whole electrical system. That upsets sensor references and can disturb idle. Cheap to find, and it fits the symptom.

**2. Ground voltage drops.** DMM on **DC millivolts**, engine idling with headlights and blower on:
- Battery negative post → engine block: **under 0.1 V**
- Engine block → chassis: **under 0.1 V**
- Battery negative post → chassis: **under 0.1 V**

Anything above that is resistance in a ground path, and it shifts every sensor reference that shares it.

**3. Sensor reference voltage.** Back-probe the 5 V reference at the throttle body or MAF connector. Should read a steady 5.00 V. Drift or ripple means a shared-reference problem affecting several sensors at once.

**4. Wiggle test.** Engine idling, rpm graph on the phone where you can see it. Firmly flex and tap — one at a time — the crank sensor connector and harness, the cam sensor connectors, the MAF connector, the coil connectors, and the main engine harness where it passes the block. **Any rpm response is your fault.** This finds intermittent connections nothing else will.

## Vacuum gauge — worth doing, but be clear what for

**Not for finding a leak.** Short-term trim sitting at ±1.5% already says there isn't one big enough to matter. Don't chase that.

**Do it to read combustion character.** The gauge responds to individual firing events — a bandwidth no scan tool reaches:

- **Steady 18–22 inHg** → cylinders contributing evenly
- **Needle drifting slowly back and forth** → the same slow disturbance we're chasing, now visible mechanically
- **Rapid regular flutter** → valve sealing
- **Periodic sharp drop** → one cylinder weaker than the rest

## Order of work

1. **Cam advance vs rpm screenshot** — you're connected, it costs two minutes
2. **AC ripple across the battery** — one probe, one number
3. **Ground drops and the wiggle test** — 20 minutes with a DMM
4. **Vacuum gauge**
5. **Cylinder balance** — unplug one injector at a time, note each rpm drop

Send the cam trace first. If that's flat and the electrical tests are clean, we go to the gauge and the balance test, and at that point we're measuring the engine directly instead of asking the PCM about it.

1. The PCM *believes* rpm is wandering
2. It modulates spark to correct a phantom
3. **That spark modulation makes the engine genuinely oscillate**
4. And we'd see exactly this data set — plus a felt roughness

That would explain why no input is moving and yet the engine won't sit still. It's the one thing that could produce all of this from a single fault.

**How to test it:** measure engine speed **independently of the PCM.**

- **Timing light with a tach function**, or an inductive tach on a plug lead. If the independent reading is steadier than the app's, the crank signal is lying.
- Same idea with the phone accelerometer: firing frequency should be rpm/60 × 3. If the measured vibration frequency is rock steady while the app's rpm wanders, the sensor is the fault.

## Your ordered plan — all physical now

**1. AC ripple across the battery.** DMM on AC volts, engine idling. Under 0.1 V AC. Above that, an alternator diode is injecting ripple into every sensor reference.

**2. Ground voltage drops.** DMM on DC millivolts, idling with loads on. Battery negative → block, block → chassis, battery negative → chassis. Each under 0.1 V.

**3. Wiggle test.** Idling, rpm graph visible. Flex and tap the **crank sensor connector and harness first**, then cam sensors, MAF, coils. Any rpm response is your fault.

**4. Independent rpm.** Timing light tach against the app's reading.

**5. Vacuum gauge.** Not for leaks — for combustion character. Steady 18–22 inHg, or a drifting needle, or a rhythmic drop.

**6. Cylinder balance.** Unplug one injector at a time, note each rpm drop. Six numbers. Unequal = your cylinder.

Tests 1–3 need only a multimeter and twenty minutes. Start there — a bad ground or a noisy crank signal would explain everything we've measured today.

**Method for all of them:** top graph `Engine RPM`, bottom graph the named sensor, warm, Park, A/C off, let it fill the screen. And note the **bottom graph's axis range** — that's how we tell real movement from rounding flicker.

## Tier 1 — do these first

| Bottom graph | What it answers |
|---|---|
| `MAF air flow rate` | **The most important one left.** Throttle is static, but MAF measures what actually *enters*. If MAF oscillates with a static throttle, air is varying through some other path — a leak, PCV flutter, a valve. If MAF is flat, air is genuinely constant and the torque variation is combustion or spark. |
| `Commanded throttle actuator` | We measured *actual* throttle. This is what the PCM **asks** for. If commanded oscillates while actual sits still, the plate is binding — a mechanical fault in the throttle body. |
| `Calculated engine load value` | Independent cross-check on airflow. Should track MAF. |
| `Lambda` (or `Air:fuel ratio`) | Actual measured mixture. Trim is flat — but is the *mixture* still swinging? |
| `Control module voltage` | Electrical instability correlating with rpm. Ties directly to the ripple/ground hypothesis. |

## Tier 2 — bank comparison and sanity

| Bottom graph | What it answers |
|---|---|
| `Short term fuel % trim - Bank 2` | We only have Bank 1. If the two banks behave differently, that localises the fault to three cylinders. |
| `Oxygen sensor 1 Wide Range Equivalence ratio` | Upstream bank 1, raw |
| `Oxygen sensor 5 Wide Range Equivalence ratio` | Upstream bank 2, raw. Compare against the previous one — a lazy or different-behaving sensor shows up here |
| `Knock retard` | Should be a flat 0. If it spikes, knock is pulling timing and that alone explains the spark swing |
| `Intake air temperature` | Sanity check — should be steady. A jumping IAT is a sensor fault |

- `Catalyst temperature Bank 1 Sensor 1` **and** `Bank 2 Sensor 1` — divergence between banks means one bank is running differently

- `Learned octane`

## Lambda is genuinely oscillating

Axis check: lambda spans **0.98 to 1.02**, AFR spans **14.37 to 14.97**. The trace fills that range with clean, smooth, large sine waves — not rounding steps, not quantisation flicker. That's a **±2% mixture swing**, and the period is ~3.4 seconds: **the same period as the rpm.**

A ±2% AFR swing at idle is more than enough to modulate torque and move the engine 20–40 rpm.

## I was wrong to eliminate fuel

I said "fuel control eliminated" because short-term trim stayed inside ±1.5%. **That was a mistake, and it's a conceptual one, not a reading error.**

Short-term trim is the *correction the PCM applies*. Lambda is *the mixture the engine actually gets*. Those are different things — and I treated a small correction as proof of a stable mixture. It isn't. The mixture is swinging 2% while the reported trim barely moves.

That's the second time in this investigation I've mistaken a flat derived value for a flat physical reality.

## What this changes

We now have **two things oscillating at the same 3.4 s period**: spark advance and lambda. Air is flat, throttle is flat, cam is parked, purge is flat.

So the chain is either:
- **Mixture swings → torque swings → rpm swings → PCM chases with spark**, or
- The PCM is *deliberately* cycling the mixture, and everything else follows

**And one detail matters a lot:** 3.4 seconds is *slow* for closed-loop cycling. Deliberate catalyst dither typically runs 0.5–2 Hz. A slow, large lambda cycle is the textbook signature of a **lazy upstream oxygen sensor** — the loop period stretches when sensor response slows.

Your O2 sensors were "cleaned" by an unknown method. I raised that, then killed it on the trim evidence. **It's back, and now there's a measurement behind it.**

Honest limit: I cannot resolve from these screenshots whether lambda leads rpm or follows it. The phase looks close and I won't guess.

**`Engine RPM` + `Fuel/Air commanded equivalence ratio`**

- **Commanded also oscillating** → the PCM is doing this on purpose. Normal fuel control, and we look elsewhere.

**Stop and check this before anything else.**

## ECU voltage is 12.6 V with the engine running

Min 12.39, max 12.82, average 12.62–12.67 across all four captures.

**That is not a charging voltage.** A healthy system at idle should sit **13.5–14.5 V**. 12.6 V is a battery with nothing replenishing it.

And earlier in this same session, your full sensor scan read **13.76 V control module, 14.0 V OBD module, 13.8 V battery**. So it *was* charging, and now it isn't.

## Two possibilities

**1. Ford's smart charging strategy.** Some Fords deliberately reduce alternator output once the battery is well charged, to save fuel. Your earlier scan showed 88% state of charge. If that's what this is, it's normal and I'll drop it.

**2. The alternator has stopped charging.** In which case you've been running the engine for well over an hour on battery alone, and it's draining.

I can't tell which from the OBD data.

The trace swings **0.43 V peak to peak** with rapid irregular spikes. The axis is zoomed — but 0.43 V is genuinely large for a supply rail, about 3.4% of 12.6 V. On a clean system you'd expect a fraction of that.

Noise on the PCM supply matters, because every sensor reference is derived from it.

===== [163] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T21:06:52.131Z =====
**That's the answer to that question: the PCM is commanding it.**

## Commanded fuel/air is a square wave

It toggles between exactly **14.41 and 14.86** — two values, nothing in between, a clean binary switch. Period roughly 3.5–4 seconds, matching everything else.

And your measured lambda/AFR (14.37–14.97, smooth sine) is that square wave **filtered by exhaust mixing and sensor response**. Measured is simply following commanded.

## So the chain is now complete

**PCM commands a ±1.5% mixture swing → torque follows the mixture → rpm swings ±20 → spark advance modulates to trim it.**

Every observation of the last two hours falls out of that one command. Nothing is disturbing your engine. **The engine is obeying instructions.**

That also means the mixture oscillation is *not* a fault — it's deliberate air-fuel dither, which is normal. The PCM cycles rich/lean around stoichiometric to keep the catalyst's oxygen storage working. Every port-injected engine does it.

## But the period is the suspicious part

Catalyst dither is normally **faster** than this. At 1–2 Hz the engine's inertia averages it out and you feel nothing. At **0.28 Hz — 3.5 seconds per cycle — the engine has time to actually follow the mixture**, and that's why you can see it in the needle.

**What sets the speed of that outer loop is the downstream oxygen sensor.** A slow rear sensor makes the loop slow, and a slow loop makes a big lazy dither the engine can track.

Your O2 sensors were "cleaned" by an unknown method — and that includes the downstream ones.

I killed the O2 hypothesis earlier on trim evidence, then reopened it on lambda. This is the third time it's come back, and now there's a specific mechanism: **not the upstream sensors mismetering, but the downstream sensors making the fuel control loop too slow.**

## Next, in this order

1. **`Oxygen sensor 2 Bank 1 Voltage`** — downstream bank 1. This is the sensor that sets the loop speed.
2. **`Oxygen sensor 2 Bank 2 Voltage`** — downstream bank 2
3. **`Oxygen sensor 1 Wide Range Equivalence ratio`** — upstream bank 1
4. **`Oxygen sensor 5 Wide Range Equivalence ratio`** — upstream bank 2

On the downstream ones I'm looking for whether they sit reasonably steady around 0.6–0.8 V, or whether they're swinging with the dither — and how sharply they respond.

## Saved for tomorrow, multimeter

- **DC volts across the battery, engine idling** — the ECU voltage reading 12.6 V still needs explaining
- **AC volts across the battery** — under 0.1 V
- **Ground drops** ×3
- **Wiggle test**, crank sensor harness first

I'll keep those on the list. Carry on with the four O2 traces.

===== [165] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T21:14:49.570Z =====
**That is the most specific finding of the entire investigation.**

## The downstream sensor is swinging full range

O2S2 bank 1: **0.17 V to 0.82 V**, clean sweeps, same 3.5–4 second period. Average 0.58–0.63.

A post-catalyst sensor should **not** do that. Its job is to look at exhaust *after* the catalyst has done its work, and a healthy catalyst stores and releases oxygen — it buffers the upstream rich/lean dither into a nearly flat downstream signal. Typical reading is a lazy 0.6–0.8 V that barely moves.

Yours is passing the full swing straight through.

**The catalyst on bank 1 is not buffering.** Its oxygen storage isn't absorbing the mixture cycling.

Note also: the sensor itself is clearly **not** lazy — it's making full transitions in under a second. So this isn't a damaged sensor. It's a working sensor reporting what's actually coming out.

## This closes the causal chain

**Catalyst not storing oxygen → downstream sensor swings full range → the PCM's fore/aft loop sees a big error and drives the mixture hard to correct → ±1.5% commanded dither → torque varies → rpm swings ±20 → spark modulates to compensate.**

Every single thing we measured today falls out of that, in order.

## The honest alternative

Your monitor status showed **"Catalyst: not completed"** — that test hasn't run since codes were cleared. And a catalyst monitor works by *deliberately* perturbing the mixture and watching the downstream sensor respond. So it's possible the PCM is actively testing the cat right now, and this is normal temporary behaviour.

Against that: your symptom predates the code clear by years. A monitor test doesn't explain something you've had since you bought the truck.

Also worth noting: you've been idling nearly an hour, and catalyst temp read 458 °C. Prolonged idle cools a cat and reduces its storage capacity. That could exaggerate what we're seeing.

## Next — and this one is important

**`Oxygen sensor 2 Bank 2 Voltage`** — the downstream sensor on the other bank.

- **Bank 2 also swinging full range** → both catalysts, or a systemic fuel-control behaviour
- **Bank 2 relatively flat while bank 1 swings** → **bank 1's catalyst specifically**, and you have a localised, named fault

That comparison is the whole question now. Send it next.

**And a prediction to check later:** if the bank 1 catalyst really is degraded, then once you drive normally and the catalyst monitor completes, expect a **P0420** to appear. That's a falsifiable test of this whole theory — and it costs nothing but time.

===== [167] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T21:16:31.267Z =====
Bank 2 is **better, but not clean** — and the asymmetry is real.

| | Bank 1 | Bank 2 |
|---|---|---|
| Average | **0.58–0.63 V** | **0.70–0.72 V** |
| Typical minimum | **0.17–0.22 V** | **0.30–0.39 V** |

| Shape | symmetric, full swing | biased high, shallower dips |

**Bank 1's post-catalyst exhaust swings deeper and sits leaner.** Bank 2 spends most of its time up at 0.78–0.80 with narrower excursions down. Both are moving more than a well-buffered catalyst should, but bank 1 is clearly the worse of the two.

## What I'm not going to claim

I can't condemn a catalyst from idle data, and I won't. Two honest reasons:

**Idle is the wrong condition.** You've been idling nearly an hour, catalyst temp read 458 °C, and exhaust flow is minimal. Catalysts work poorly at extended idle and the monitor deliberately doesn't run there.

**And the logic is circular from here.** Either the catalyst is weak and the fuel loop chases it with a big dither — or the PCM is commanding a big dither for some other reason and it's punching through a catalyst that's actually fine. Idle data can't separate those.

There's also something that doesn't fit my own theory: a catalyst with *less* storage should make the loop cycle **faster**, not slower. Yours is slow at 3.5–4 s. That's the opposite of what a degraded cat predicts, and I'd be glossing over it if I didn't say so.

## The test that resolves it — and it needs driving

**Steady cruise, 60–80 km/h, watch `Engine RPM` + `O2S2 volt. (B1)`.** Then repeat for B2.

- **Downstream goes flat at cruise** → catalysts are fine, and the idle dither is something else
- **Downstream still swinging at cruise** → the catalysts are genuinely degraded, bank 1 first

At cruise a healthy catalyst holds that sensor nearly still. This is the standard way the condition is judged, and it's the only version of this test that means anything.

**Second thing to watch on that drive:** does the mixture dither continue at cruise? You've told me the shake is absent under load. If the dither carries on while the shake doesn't, then the dither isn't what you're feeling either — and that's worth knowing.

## Where we are

Your phone's at 19%. Good place to stop.

Today established a complete causal chain: **commanded mixture dither → torque varies → rpm oscillates ±20 → spark modulates to compensate.** Air, throttle, purge and cam are all eliminated by measurement. And you've found a real bank asymmetry in the downstream sensors.

**Same fuel going in. Different exhaust coming out.**

Both banks get the same commanded dither, both upstream sensors report it faithfully and fast. The *only* asymmetry in the entire dataset sits downstream — which means it's in what lies **between** the upstream and downstream sensors on bank 1. That's the bank 1 catalyst.

## 2. Key Technical Concepts

- OBD-II services: Mode 01 (live data), 03/07/0A (stored/pending/permanent DTCs), 06 (monitor tests), 09 (VIN/CalIDs), 22 (manufacturer ReadDataByIdentifier)
- ELM327 serial transport; ISO 15765-4 CAN; serial ports are **exclusive** (one process at a time)
- PID quantisation: fuel trim 0.78%/LSB, percent PIDs 0.392%/LSB (100/255), MAF 0.01 g/s
- Closed-loop fuel control, catalyst oxygen-storage dither, fore/aft (upstream/downstream) O2 control loop
- Wideband upstream sensors (equivalence ratio), narrowband downstream (voltage)
- Engine orders: at 660 rpm a V6 fires at ~33 Hz (3/rev), crank 11 Hz, cam 5.5 Hz; the observed hunt is ~0.28 Hz
- Choked flow through a fixed throttle at idle (MAP ~30 kPa vs 100 kPa baro) → MAF independent of engine speed
- Ti-VCT internal EGR via cam overlap (no external EGR valve on the 3.7)
- Autocorrelation for periodicity (amplitude-gated), cross-correlation for lead/lag
- Python: dataclasses, restricted-AST expression evaluator (never `eval`), YAML-driven protocol graphs

## Start with this question, because it changes item 3

Every graph you've sent shows **two channels: `Engine RPM` plus one sensor.**

**Can you choose *both* channels?** Or is `Engine RPM` fixed as the first one and you only pick the second?

If you can pick both, then item 3 is this, and it is worth more than most of the list:

> **`O2S1 air:fuel` (upstream bank 1) paired with `O2S2 Bank 1 Voltage` (downstream bank 1)** — in the same window.

**Why this matters.** Upstream is what goes *into* the catalyst. Downstream is what comes *out*. Put them on one time axis and you can see the catalyst working: how much the dither gets **flattened**, and how much it gets **delayed**. That delay *is* the oxygen storage capacity — it is the actual measurement, not an inference from two separate recordings taken minutes apart.

**Why this is the strongest test left.** Upstream is what goes *into* the catalyst; downstream is what comes *out*. On one time axis, three things become measurable that separate recordings cannot show:

- **Attenuation** — how much smaller the downstream swing is than the upstream one. A healthy catalyst absorbs most of the dither.
- **Delay** — how far the downstream curve lags the upstream one. That lag *is* the oxygen storage. It's the actual physical quantity, measured, not inferred.
- **Shape** — whether downstream is a smoothed version of upstream, or is doing something of its own.

Bank 1's downstream sensor swings deeper and leaner than bank 2's. This tells me whether that's because bank 1's catalyst stores less oxygen, or because bank 1's *mixture* was different in that window. Two separate recordings could never distinguish those.

The upstream sensor reads the mixture *after* the PCM has corrected it. The trim reads *how much correction was needed*. Identical lambda with different trims means the underlying fuelling is **not** identical — bank 2 arrives leaner and the PCM makes up the difference.

### And it agrees with the downstream finding

Bank 2 gets more fuel added → its exhaust runs marginally richer → its downstream sensor sits higher (0.70–0.72 V vs bank 1's 0.58–0.63 V). Two independent measurements, same direction. That makes both more credible.

### How much does 3.5 % matter?

**On its own, not much.** Anything within ±10 % is normal, and a few points of bank-to-bank spread is common — intake manifold air distribution alone can do it. This is not a fault by itself and it will not throw a code.

What makes it worth pursuing is that it's now the **second** consistent asymmetry, and the truck's whole symptom profile (worst at idle, gone under load) matches the things that cause a bank-specific lean bias *at idle only*: a small vacuum leak feeding one bank, or an exhaust leak upstream of the bank 2 sensor drawing air in during the low-flow pulsations at idle and sealing itself under load.

I'm not claiming either. I'm saying the shape fits, which nothing else has.

**Why this separates the candidates cleanly.** The three explanations for a bank-specific lean bias behave completely differently when airflow triples:

| Cause | What happens to the +3.5 % gap at 2000 rpm |
|---|---|
| **Vacuum leak feeding bank 2** | **Shrinks sharply**, toward zero. A fixed leak is a big fraction of idle airflow and a small fraction of 2000 rpm airflow. |
| **Exhaust leak before the B2 sensor** | **Shrinks or reverses.** Higher exhaust pressure stops air being drawn in through the leak. |
| **Sensor bias, injector mismatch, or manifold air distribution** | **Stays about the same.** These are proportional errors — they don't care about flow rate. |

That is a genuine fork in the road, it costs nothing, and it uses the truck's own load curve — the one behaviour this symptom has always shown. If the gap collapses at 2000 rpm, we're looking for a leak on bank 2 and I can tell you exactly where to look. If it holds at 3.5 %, leaks are out and it's the sensor or the fuelling hardware.

This is still a stationary test in Park, so it doesn't break your "finish idle before cruising" rule.

| What you see at 2000 rpm | What it means |
|---|---|

| Both stay **near zero** | **Not a leak.** The +3 % is needed at every speed, which means the computer's air measurement is slightly off — the MAF sensor or the pressure reading. Not a hole. |

Same fork: if the driving number is near 0 while idle sits at +3 %, it's a leak at idle. If both are +3 %, it's the air measurement.

I'd said a weak cylinder would show up at 33 Hz in the accelerometer test. **That's wrong.** One weak cylinder repeats once per *full engine cycle* — half crank speed, about **5.5 Hz** at your idle. 33 Hz is the normal firing beat of a healthy V6. Fixed in the repo.

===== [196] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T22:15:42.369Z =====
LTFT B2 is a **flat line at 2.34 %** — min, max and average all identical across 70 seconds. That's a solid, settled number.

But STFT B1 is doing something worth catching:

| Window (graph clock) | STFT B1 centre |
|---|---|
| 45:35 – 45:50 | ~**0** (swinging −1.56 to +1.56) |
| 45:55 – 46:12 | ~**−1.3** |
| 46:15 – 46:30 | ~**−1.4** |
| 46:28 – 46:43 | ~**−1.6** |

**Bank 1's short-term trim is walking steadily negative over 70 seconds.** That is not a fault — it's the two trims handing off to each other, and it changes how you read every trim number in this project.

## The rule I should have given you earlier

**Short term and long term trim are two halves of one number.**

```
Total fuel correction = Short term + Long term
```

Long term is the slow learned value. Short term is the fast correction on top of it. **When long term rises, short term falls by the same amount** — the engine still needs the same total, it's just been moved from the fast pot to the slow pot.

At 01:00: Long term B1 = **+3.13**, Short term B1 = **0** → total **+3.1 %**
At 01:13: Short term B1 = **−1.4** → Long term B1 must have climbed to about **+4.5** → total still **~+3.1 %**

**The total hasn't moved. The engine still wants +3 %.** All that happened is the computer got more confident and moved the correction into long-term memory.

This also means **neither number alone tells you anything.** Every trim reading from here needs both halves of the same bank.

**It came back to exactly 2.34, instantly.** Not 0 climbing back up — *exactly* the number it left. That proves these are **two separate learned memories**, one for idle and one for higher load, and the computer just switches between them.

## What that means

| Condition | Learned correction, bank 2 |
|---|---|
| **Idle** | **+2.34 %** — adding fuel |
| **~2000 rpm** | **0 %** — adding nothing |

**The engine runs lean at idle and stops running lean the moment the throttle opens.**

That is the signature of **air getting in past the airflow sensor** — a vacuum leak. The reason it only shows at idle is simple arithmetic: a fixed-size hole is a big share of the tiny amount of air the engine breathes at idle, and almost nothing next to what it breathes at 2000 rpm.

## And look at your own symptom table

| Condition | Manifold vacuum | Your shake |
|---|---|---|
| P / N standstill | Highest — leak pulls hardest | **Worst** |
| D / R standstill | Slightly lower | **Less** |
| Driving | Lowest — leak irrelevant | **Gone** |

**That is the same curve.** Highest vacuum, worst shake. Throttle open, symptom gone. For the first time in this whole investigation, a measured finding has the same shape as the complaint.

Let me get this recorded properly.

===== [200] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T22:26:52.557Z =====
Pushed — `5fce2db`. Now the honest part, and then what to do.

## The one thing that could still undo this

`LTFT - B2` read exactly **0.00** in the higher-rpm memory. Two readings:

1. **It learned zero** — no correction needed at 2000 rpm. That's the leak.
2. **It never learned** — that memory is just sitting at its factory default of 0.

Only **101 km and 3 warm-ups** have passed since the codes were wiped, while the idle memory got three solid hours tonight. So reading 2 is genuinely possible and I'm not going to pretend otherwise.

**The drive settles it.** After 15–20 minutes of real driving that memory will be properly learned. If it's *still* near zero while idle sits at +2.3 to +3.1 %, the leak is confirmed. If it climbs to +3 % as well, then the correction is needed everywhere — and it's the airflow sensor or the pressure reading, not a hole.

I'm also not using `STFT B1` from the hold for anything. Wrong pairing — bank 1's short-term against bank 2's long-term gives me neither bank's total — and you were holding the throttle by hand with the revs unrecorded, so drift alone could explain its wander.

## Next, in order

### 1. One capture to complete the picture

**`Long term fuel % trim - Bank 1` and `Long term fuel % trim - Bank 2`, together.**

Idle for a window, then 2000 rpm for a window, then idle again. Both banks, both memories, one setup.

I already know bank 1's idle memory: **+3.13 %**. What I don't know is bank 1's higher-rpm memory. If it drops to ~0 the same way bank 2 did, both sides of the engine are telling the same story and there's nothing left to argue about.

### 2. The drive

15–20 minutes, with a good stretch of steady 60–80 km/h. Then read those same two channels while cruising.

That does three jobs at once: it settles the un-learned question, it gives me the real cruise trims, and it makes the truck run its own converter and oxygen sensor tests, which have been showing **"Not completed"** all night.

### 3. Then the smoke test

**I was wrong earlier when I said trims didn't justify one.** That was right when long-term read 0 % and I thought it hadn't learned. It reads +2.34 % at idle and 0 % at load now, and that's exactly the pattern a smoke test is built to find.

Where to smoke, in order of how likely and how untouched:

| Where | Why it's on the list |
|---|---|
| **PCV valve, hose, grommet, elbow** | Never inspected. Twelve years of Jeddah heat. The plastic elbows crack. |
| **Brake booster line and check valve** | Never tested |
| **Purge valve and its line** | Never touched — and purge is commanded ~40 % at idle, so this circuit is live |
| **Intake manifold gasket** | Composite manifold, twelve years |
| **Throttle body gasket, injector O-rings** | Both joints were opened during earlier work |

Note on those last two: they **can't be what started this** — you've said the shake was there before any repair. But a joint that's been opened can leak now regardless of what caused the original problem.

## What I'm not claiming

**+2.34 % is a small leak.** On its own it would not normally shake a cab.

What makes it worth chasing is that a leak is rarely evenly shared. If it feeds one intake runner more than the others, that one cylinder runs noticeably leaner while the bank *average* only moves two or three percent — far too small to set a code. That gives you a weak power stroke once per engine cycle, about **5.5 Hz** at your idle. Which is exactly what the accelerometer test looks for, and what the injector-kill test would name.

So: find the leak first. Then see whether fixing it changes what you feel.

## And there's something extra in this capture

Look at bank 1 during the hold. It isn't pinned at 0 — it keeps flicking up to **0.78 %** and back. Bank 2 does it too, less often.

Because you couldn't hold 2000 exactly, the revs wandered — and each time they wandered, the computer crossed into a **neighbouring memory cell**. Those cells hold **0.78 %**.

So the learned correction isn't two values. It's a **slope**:

| Where the engine is | Learned correction |
|---|---|
| Idle | **+3.13 / +2.34 %** |
| Just above idle | **+0.78 %** |
| ~2000 rpm | **0 %** |

**The correction fades away smoothly as the engine breathes more air.** That is what a fixed-size hole does — it's a big share of a little airflow and a tiny share of a lot. A wrong airflow sensor or a wrong pressure reading would put **the same number in every cell**, because it's a percentage error, not a fixed hole.

That slope is better evidence than the two endpoints alone.

## Your observation about the revs is worth recording

You said something was moving the rpm up and down while you held the throttle. Two things make that expected rather than sinister on this truck:

- **There's no cable.** Your pedal is a sensor. The computer decides the actual throttle angle — a steady foot does not mean a steady throttle plate.
- **Coolant was at 98 °C.** The cooling fan cycling on and off at that temperature loads and unloads the engine noticeably.

Not dismissing it — it's worth a proper look later with `Engine RPM` and `Throttle Position Actually` on screen together at 2000 rpm. But it doesn't touch tonight's finding, because that rests on the learned cell values, which don't care how steady your foot was.

===== [203] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T22:37:24.878Z =====
Pushed — `e6abfee`.

## The prime suspect, and it fits the arithmetic exactly

`Commanded evaporative purge` has been sitting at **~40 %, flat**, all night — on a truck that had been idling **over three hours**. A canister should be long empty of fuel vapour by then.

**Purge flow enters the intake after the air sensor.** So if the canister is dry, what's flowing through that valve is **plain air the computer never measured**.

Now the fraction:

| | Total air the engine takes | Same purge flow is... |
|---|---|---|
| **Idle** | ~3 g/s | a **big** share |
| **2000 rpm** | several times that | a **small** share |

**That is the slope you just measured.** Not similar to it — that *is* it.

And this circuit has never been touched in twelve years. The Mustang 3.7 crowd — same engine — names stuck-open as *the* failure mode for Ford's purge valve, and it usually sets no code.

## The test — and it respects your rule about the hard lines

Nothing gets disconnected with the engine running.

**1.** Engine **OFF**.

**2.** Disconnect the purge line **at the intake manifold** end.

**3.** **Plug the manifold port properly.** It has to seal — a bolt with a bit of tape, a golf tee, a proper cap. If it leaks you're just swapping one leak for another.

**4.** **Leave the purge valve's electrical plug connected.** That way no circuit code gets set and there's nothing to clear afterwards.

**5.** Restart. Give it 2–3 minutes to settle back into closed loop.

**6.** Watch **`Short term fuel % trim - Bank 1`** and **`Short term fuel % trim - Bank 2`**.

### What tells you the answer

Long-term is still holding **+3.13 / +2.34** in memory — it won't drop straight away, it learns slowly. So short-term is what moves.

| What short-term does | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **Falls to about −3 % and stays** | **Purge was the source.** The engine no longer needs the fuel that long-term is still adding, so short-term takes it back out. |
| **Stays around 0** | Purge is clear. Move to the next line. |

That's an unambiguous, fast answer.

## If purge is clean, same method, in this order

| Next | Why |
|---|---|
| **PCV valve, hose, grommet, elbow** | Never inspected, twelve years of Jeddah heat, and the elbows go hard and crack |
| **Brake booster line and check valve** | Never tested |

Same procedure every time: engine off, disconnect, **plug the manifold port**, restart, read both short-term trims.

## If all three are clean

Then it's a leak in a joint rather than a line — manifold gasket, throttle body gasket, injector seats — and **that's when the smoke test earns its money**, because those can't be isolated by unplugging anything.

Two of those joints were opened during your earlier work. They can't be what *started* this, since the shake predates all of it — but a joint that's been opened can leak now regardless.

===== [204] USER 2026-09-04T22:42:32.411Z =====
purge is mounted on intake should i remove it

===== [205] ASSISTANT 2026-09-04T22:43:31.476Z =====
**No — don't remove it.** You don't need to open the manifold at all, and doing so just creates a joint you have to reseal.

The valve has two sides: the manifold side (where it's mounted) and the **inlet nipple** with the hose running back to the canister. **Block the inlet.** If nothing can get *in*, it doesn't matter whether the valve is stuck open — there's nothing for the manifold to pull.

## Best option, if you have a hand vacuum pump

This tests the valve directly. Yes or no, no trims involved.

1. Engine **off**
2. Unplug the valve's electrical connector — de-energised, it should spring **shut**
3. Pull the hose off the **inlet nipple** (canister side)
4. Hand pump on that nipple, pull about **20 inHg / 500 mmHg**
5. **It must hold.**

| Result | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Holds steady** | Valve seals. It's not your leak. Move to PCV. |
| **Bleeds down** | **Valve is stuck open.** That's the answer — done. |

Plug the connector back in before you restart, so no code gets set.

Mustang 3.7 sources say stuck-open is *the* way Ford's purge valve fails, and it usually throws no code at all — which is exactly the situation you're in.

## No pump? Then block it and read the trims

1. Engine **off**
2. Pull the hose off the **inlet nipple**
3. **Cap the nipple.** The loose hose end can stay open — it only goes back to the canister, which is at atmosphere anyway.
4. **Leave the electrical connector plugged in** — that way nothing sets a code
5. Restart, give it **2–3 minutes** to settle into closed loop
6. Read **`Short term fuel % trim - Bank 1`** and **`Short term fuel % trim - Bank 2`**

Long-term still holds **+3.13 / +2.34** in memory and won't drop for a while, so short-term is what moves:

| Short-term does this | Verdict |
|---|---|
| **Goes to about −3 % and stays** | **Purge was feeding unmetered air.** The engine no longer needs what long-term is still adding, so short-term pulls it back out. |
| **Stays around 0** | Purge is clean. Next line. |

## While it's capped — the test that actually matters

Sit in the cab for a minute. **Does the shake change? Does the needle stop breathing?**

The trims tell me whether I found a leak. That tells you whether the leak is your problem. They're not the same question, and yours is the one worth answering.

It delivers fuel vapour from a canister that's had years to load up while the old valve scavenged it dry. At idle that vapour is a bigger share of a small airflow, so the mixture runs slightly rich there and neutral in Drive. That's a purge valve doing its job, not a fault.

## So here is where this actually stands

I'm going to say this plainly rather than hedge:

1. There **was** a real fault — a lean condition at idle, +3.1 % and +2.3 %, fading to zero as airflow rose.

3. **The D and R shake went away with it.**
4. **The P and N shake did not.**
5. The fuel system is now measurably clean in the exact condition that still shakes.

**Fuelling does not explain what's left.** That's not "we found nothing" — it's a proper elimination. We found something, fixed it, watched the number move, and the remaining symptom didn't care.

## And the pattern you're left with is what every automatic does

Rough in P and N, smooth in D and R at a standstill — that is **normal torque-converter behaviour**. In gear, the converter loads the engine and damps the firing pulses. Every automatic is smoother in gear standing still. This project noted that early and then partly forgot it.

The most likely reading now: **the purge leak was pushing the shake above the threshold where you could feel it in D and R. Remove it, and what's left is the baseline of a 60° V6 in a bare regular cab at its highest-vacuum, lowest-load condition.**

Not proven. But nothing in the data argues against it any more, and the "is this a fault at all" question — open since the first night — has never been answered because **nobody has ever compared this truck to another 3.7.**

## Three tests can still change that answer. Nothing on OBD can.

**1. `Engine RPM` + `Timing advance`, in Park then in Drive** — finish A3 and B3. Two minutes, and it's the last cheap thing that could surprise us. If the needle breathes in Park and goes still in Drive, the hunt and the shake are one thing.

**2. Phone accelerometer on the seat, in Park and in Drive.** This measures your actual complaint instead of a proxy.

| Frequency | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ~**33 Hz** | Normal V6 firing pulse through a bare floor. Nothing to fix. |
| ~**11 Hz** | Rotational imbalance |
| ~**5.5 Hz** | **One cylinder differing from the other five** |

**3. Cylinder balance — unplug one injector at a time, note each rpm drop.** Six numbers. Equal drops means all six pull their weight. One small drop names the cylinder.

Then the control sample on another 3.7, which settles the whole thing in two minutes.

The scan tool has given everything it has. **From here the answer comes from measuring the vibration itself, or from another truck.**

| **Now** | 0 (wiped) | **−1.5 %** | **−1.5 %** — removing fuel |

**That swing of ~4.6 points is real and the wipe doesn't touch it.** The engine genuinely no longer runs lean at idle. My reading of the repair stands; only the long-term numbers behind it were bad.

## One honest complication

The new valve went in **and** the adaptives were wiped, at the same moment. So the D/R improvement has two candidate explanations, not one.

Against the reset being the cause: **you did a relearn plus 300 km before all this and nothing changed.** A reset alone has already been tried and failed. That points firmly at the valve.

But it isn't a clean experiment, and the way to close it is simply to let the adaptives relearn and see whether D/R stays clean. **If the improvement was from the reset, the shake will come back in D and R as long term learns.**

**30 minutes minimum**, mixed, with a good stretch of steady 60–80 km/h. That does four jobs at once — relearns long term, runs the catalyst and oxygen sensor monitors, refills Mode 06, and tells you whether D/R stays clean.

## What this settles

**The hunt and the shake are the same thing.**

They appear together in Park. They vanish together in Drive. Since the first night I've been saying they were probably two separate phenomena — a slow breathing the scan tool sees, and a fast vibration it can't. **That's now unlikely. There is one thing to explain, not two.**

A ±25 rpm swing repeating every 3.4 seconds is a 4 % cycling of engine speed. That's exactly what a tachometer needle "breathing" looks like, and it's exactly what would be felt in a bare cab as a rhythmic unevenness — without ever loping, stumbling, or sounding wrong from under the hood.

## What it does not settle

**The Park-versus-Drive difference is also what a healthy automatic does.**

In Drive the torque converter loads the engine. A loaded engine is far better damped — the same torque disturbance produces much less speed variation. A control loop that limit-cycles unloaded in Park can be perfectly stable in gear. That's normal on every torque-converter automatic, and this file has said so before.

So the honest reading:

- The PCM's own fuel dither (measured: a ±1.5 % AFR square wave at 3.4 s) produces a torque ripple.
- **In Park** — unloaded, low inertia — it shows as **±25 rpm**.
- **In Drive** — loaded and damped — it shows as **±8 rpm**.
- Your purge leak was adding to the disturbance. Removing it dropped D/R below what you can feel. Park is still above it.

**Whether ±25 rpm in Park is abnormal for a 3.7 remains genuinely unknown**, because nobody has ever measured another one.

===== [225] ASSISTANT 2026-09-05T00:30:47.915Z =====
You're right and I was leaning on a weak inference. Let me correct it properly.

**A ±25 rpm breathing every 3.4 seconds does not shake you in the seat.** It moves a needle. It doesn't move a person. If what you feel is strong enough to be felt through the seat base, it isn't that.

## Where my reasoning went wrong

I concluded the hunt and the shake were one thing because they both go away in Drive. **That's weak evidence, and I should have caught it:** the torque converter damps *everything* when you put it in gear — the slow speed oscillation and the per-firing vibration alike. So "both disappear in D" doesn't tell them apart at all. Withdrawn.

## And this un-eliminates something I wrote off

This file has mounts marked "ruled out," on the grounds that D and R feel the same as each other. **That argument only rules out torque-reaction faults** — a mount that's collapsed on one side would load differently in D than in R.

**It does not rule out a mount that has lost its damping.** That failure isn't direction-dependent, so the D-versus-R test is blind to it.

And the pattern fits uncomfortably well:

- The engine rocks on its mounts at its own natural frequency at idle — typically **8–15 Hz**, right in the range you feel through a seat rather than hear.
- Fluid-filled mounts exist specifically to damp that mode. When one loses its fluid, the engine rocks harder and the cab gets it.
- **In gear, converter drag preloads the engine against the mounts**, shifting it a few millimetres and changing that rocking mode entirely.

Worst in P/N, gone in D/R, felt not heard, no codes, unchanged by six repairs to fuel and ignition. That's the shape.

## And a second candidate I should have raised much earlier

**Something touching the body.** An exhaust pipe, an A/C line, a power steering line, a wiring loom, a heat shield — anything resting against the frame or cab.

When you shift into gear the engine rotates slightly on its mounts. **A part that just touches at rest can break contact.** That would give you: strong in P, gone in D, felt not heard, invisible to every sensor on the truck, present since you bought it, and completely unaffected by plugs, injectors, throttle body, coolant, and fluid changes.

Nothing in three nights of scan data argues against either of these. The scan tool was never going to see them.

## Do these tonight — all free, all hands-on

**1. Touch test across the mount.** Engine idling in Park. Hand flat on the engine (valve cover or block). Then hand on the frame rail right beside the mount. Then on the cab floor.

| What you feel | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Big drop from engine to frame | Mount is isolating. Doing its job. |
| **Frame feels nearly as bad as the engine** | **The mount is passing it straight through.** |

**2. Watch the engine rock.** Someone stands in front, you shift P → D → R → P. How far does it move, does it settle straight, does one side lift more than the other? On a good set of mounts it's a small, damped settle. A loose or dead mount gives a big lurch and a wobble.

**3. Hunt for contact.** Idling in Park, run your hand along everything that passes near the frame or cab — exhaust downpipe and mid-pipe, A/C lines, power steering lines, transmission cooler lines, wiring looms, heat shields. **You're feeling for one spot that's buzzing much harder than its neighbours.** Then try pushing or pulling the suspect part while someone sits in the cab and says whether the seat changes.

**4. Pry bar under the engine, gently, while it idles.** Take a little weight off each mount in turn. If the shake changes when you unload one mount, that's your mount.

**5. Then the accelerometer** — and now it's naming a family, not confirming a story:

| Frequency you measure | What it is |
|---|---|
| **~10 Hz** | **Engine rocking on its mounts** — mount damping |
| ~11 Hz | Rotational imbalance — damper, pulley, flexplate |
| ~5.5 Hz | One cylinder differing from the other five |
| ~33 Hz | Firing pulse — and then it's isolation, not the engine |

Let me record the correction.

find the issue from ecu

===== [228] ASSISTANT 2026-09-05T00:33:50.667Z =====
Fair. Let me be precise about what the ECU still holds that we have never asked it for — because there is a real gap, and it's exactly the gap that matters now.

## The ECU has cylinder-level data. We have never read it.

Everything we've pulled so far is **engine-average**: fuel trims, lambda, timing, MAF, rpm. Those tell you how the engine is doing as a whole. **None of them can see one cylinder pulling less than the other five** — the oxygen sensor averages a whole bank, the trims average a whole bank, the rpm averages the whole engine.

There are three places in the ECU that hold per-cylinder data, and **all three are still unread on your truck.**

## 1. Mode 06 — on-board monitoring test results ⭐

This is the one. The ECU keeps **per-cylinder misfire counts** here, as raw numbers with their pass/fail limits.

**Not a live-data channel — it's a separate menu.** In Car Scanner look for **"On-board monitoring tests"**, "Test results", or "Mode 06".

You'll get a list of test IDs with a value, a minimum and a maximum. **The misfire block gives one row per cylinder.** Six rows, six numbers. If one cylinder is misfiring 20× more than its neighbours — still far below the level that sets a code — that row shows it and nothing else on the truck will.

The misfire monitor reads **Completed** in your monitor list, so this data exists right now.

### And here's a correction you should have from me

I've been leaning on "**Misfire monitor: Completed, DTC count 0**" as if it meant the cylinders are healthy. **It doesn't.**

That monitor only flags misfire above roughly **2 %** — the level that hurts emissions. A cylinder producing **10 % less torque than the others while never actually misfiring** sails through it with a clean pass. That is precisely the fault that would shake a cab at idle and never set a code, and the pass tells you nothing about it.

**Mode 06 gives the actual counts instead of the pass/fail.**

## 2. Search the sensor list for "misfire"

Your app has Ford-specific channels — it's giving you `PCM Odometer`, `Learned octane`, `Knock retard`, `ATF temperature`, `Throttle Position Desired`. Some Ford profiles also carry **live misfire counters per cylinder**.

**Type "misfire" into the sensor search.** If anything comes back — `Misfire cylinder 1`, `Misfire counter`, anything — that's a live per-cylinder readout and it's the single most valuable channel on this truck.

## 3. Permanent DTCs and freeze frame

**Permanent codes (Mode 0A)** — the one code history that survives a memory wipe. Yours was wiped; permanent codes would not have been. Never read on this truck.

**Freeze frame** — a snapshot the ECU stores when a fault sets. Never read.

Both are separate menus, both take seconds.

## If the app can't do Mode 06 properly

**FORScan on a laptop can**, and it goes further: Ford's own misfire counters, and a **cylinder contribution test** where the ECU measures how much each cylinder accelerates the crankshaft. That's the ECU doing your injector-kill test for you, in software, with numbers.

The repo is already built around driving FORScan — `docs/FORSCAN.md`.

## What the ECU genuinely cannot tell you

I have to be straight about this too. **A mount that's stopped isolating, or a pipe touching the body, produces no ECU signature at all.** There is no sensor watching for it. If that's what this turns out to be, no amount of scanning will find it — which is exactly why I asked for the touch test.

**But cylinder balance is ECU-visible, and it's the one remaining cause of a strong idle vibration with no codes.** So do Mode 06 first.

Send me the misfire rows and I'll tell you whether one cylinder is different from the other five.

## The three that matter most

### ⭐ Test 3 — the neutral coast

**At 60 km/h on a clear road, shift to Neutral and coast. Then feel the seat.**

The engine is now in *exactly* the state it's in when parked — idle, unloaded, same speed. The only thing that changed is that the truck is moving.

| | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Shake is there, same as Park** | It's the engine. Mounts, cylinder balance, or the engine itself. |
| **Shake is gone** | The engine is identical and you can't feel it. So **something about standing still is letting the vibration into the cab** — exhaust or a line touching, suspension unloaded, a mount sitting differently. |

Nothing else you can do separates the *source* from the *path* that cleanly. It costs nothing and it's new — I should have thought of it days ago.

### ⭐ Test 2 — deceleration fuel cut

**Coast from 80 down to 30 in gear, foot completely off.** The computer shuts every injector off. Nothing should be burning.

Watch `Oxygen sensor 1 Wide Range Equivalence ratio`. It should **peg hard lean within a second or two.** If it hangs near 14.7, fuel is still getting in — a **leaking injector**, which would make one cylinder rich, give an uneven power stroke, and never set a code. That's exactly the profile we're chasing, and this is a free test for it.

### ⭐ Test 6 — wide open throttle

The only test that asks the engine to breathe as hard as it can.

Watch **`Absolute load value`**. On a healthy naturally aspirated engine it should climb toward **90–100 %**. **Below about 80 % means the engine isn't breathing** — restricted exhaust, blocked catalyst, cam timing, or wear.

Worth doing on your truck specifically because bank 1's after-converter sensor behaved differently from bank 2's. **A partly blocked converter chokes the engine at high flow and is completely invisible at idle.**

## The rest

Cruise trims (rebuilds the load-cell table the wipe erased) · both catalysts judged at real flow · engine-speed vs road-speed separation · converter lockup · knock under load · electrical load · A/C on and off.

**Then, before it cools:** the monitor block should flip to Completed — and **Mode 06 finally has per-cylinder misfire counts in it**, which is the only place in the ECU that can show one cylinder differing from the other five.

| App name | Where it sits |
|---|---|
| `Oxygen sensor 1 Wide Range Equivalence ratio` | **Before** the converter, one side |

===== [244] ASSISTANT 2026-09-05T01:13:04.553Z =====
Both objections are correct, and the second one hadn't occurred to me. **The test is bad as designed** — Ford raises idle in Neutral above a road-speed threshold, so the engine isn't in the same state at all, and 60 km/h of road and tyre noise swamps a small idle vibration. Withdrawn.

Here's a better replacement, and it's stationary.

## The rpm sweep in Park — do this instead

Warm, in Park, hold each of these steady for 20–30 seconds and note **how strong the shake feels in the seat** on a scale of your own:

**650 (idle) → 800 → 900 → 1000 → 1200 → 1500 → 1800**

**Why it discriminates.** Your V6 produces vibration at several frequencies at once, and they scale with rpm at different rates:

| Order | At 650 rpm | At 1200 rpm |
|---|---|---|
| Half order (one cylinder differing) | 5.4 Hz | 10 Hz |
| First order (rotational imbalance) | **10.8 Hz** | 20 Hz |
| Firing pulse (3rd order) | 32.5 Hz | 60 Hz |

**The engine rocking on its mounts resonates at roughly 8–15 Hz.** So which order is exciting that resonance depends entirely on rpm — and sweeping the rpm sweeps each order through the resonance band in turn.

| What you feel as rpm rises | What it means |
|---|---|
| **Worst at idle, clearly fading by 900–1000** | First order is driving the mount rock mode. **Mount, or rotational imbalance** — damper, pulley, flexplate. |
| **Gets noticeably worse somewhere around 1000–1800, then fades** | **Half order** — one cylinder contributing differently from the other five. Sweeping *into* the resonance. |
| **Steadily reduces, no particular peak** | Normal. Idle is simply the roughest point any engine runs at. |
| **Grows continuously with rpm** | Rotational imbalance driven directly, not through a resonance. |

Those four behaviours are genuinely different and you can feel the difference. No tools, no phone, five minutes.

## Two more that replace what the coast was meant to do

**Electrical load at idle in Park.** Headlights on, blower on maximum, rear demister, everything. That puts real drag on the engine through the alternator — a mild version of what the converter does in Drive.

**If the shake reduces**, then it's about *load damping*, which fits the whole D/R story and points at the disturbance being ordinary torque ripple rather than a broken part. **If nothing changes**, load isn't the mechanism and the D/R difference is coming from somewhere else.

**The touch test.** Still not done, and it's the direct measurement of the path — hand on the engine, then on the frame rail beside the mount, then on the cab floor. That's what the neutral coast was a poor substitute for.

Let me log the correction.

===== [246] USER 2026-09-05T01:23:04.123Z =====
worst idle at 650

===== [247] ASSISTANT 2026-09-05T01:24:01.774Z =====
That result points somewhere specific. But first I need one clarification, because two different answers both feel like "worst at idle":

**Between 650 and 900 — does it drop off sharply, or just taper gradually? And is it still noticeable at 1200 and 1500, or effectively gone?**

- **Sharp drop, gone by ~900** → a **resonance**. Something has a natural frequency right at idle.
- **Gradual taper, still a bit there at 1200** → just normal idle roughness. Every engine is roughest at its lowest speed.

## What the answer already rules out

**It did not get worse anywhere between 1000 and 1800.** That was the signature of **one weak cylinder** — half-order vibration sweeping up into the mount resonance band. It didn't happen.

That's real evidence against the single-weak-cylinder theory. Not conclusive — Mode 06 still settles it directly — but the prediction failed, and that counts.

**It also didn't grow with rpm**, which rules out imbalance driving the cab directly.

## The leading mechanical explanation now

At **650 rpm, first order is 10.8 Hz.** The engine rocking on its mounts resonates at roughly **8–15 Hz**. Your idle sits **dead centre of that band.**

As revs rise, first order climbs out of the band and the response falls away. Put it in gear and converter drag preloads the engine against the mounts, shifting the whole mode and damping it.

**That single mechanism explains every observation:**

| Observation | Explained |
|---|---|
| Worst at idle | First order at 10.8 Hz sits in the mount resonance |
| Better as rpm rises | First order climbs out of the band |
| Gone in D and R | Converter preload changes and damps the mode |
| No codes, nothing on any sensor | Mounts have no sensor |
| Present since purchase | Nothing you've done would change it |
| Six repairs changed nothing | They were all fuel and ignition |

**Where first-order vibration comes from:** a 60° V6 has an inherent once-per-revolution rocking couple. It's normally absorbed by the crankshaft counterweights, the harmonic damper, and the mounts. If a mount stops damping, that couple reaches the cab.

## Two things to check — both physical, both quick

### 1. The harmonic balancer (crank pulley damper) ⭐

Twelve years of Jeddah heat is exactly what kills these. The rubber bonding the outer ring to the hub degrades, the ring creeps or shifts, and **first-order imbalance appears out of nothing.**

- **Engine off:** look at the pulley edge-on. Is the outer ring sitting concentric with the hub? Any rubber cracked, bulging, or squeezed out of the gap? Is the timing mark still where it should be relative to the keyway?
- **Engine idling:** shine a light on it and watch. A good damper spins true. **A failed one visibly wobbles or runs eccentric.** A timing light makes it obvious — the mark will jump around instead of sitting still.

This is a genuinely common failure, it produces exactly first-order vibration, and it's a ten-minute look.

### 2. The mount touch test — still not done

Idling in Park: hand on the engine, then on the frame rail beside the mount, then on the cab floor.

**Big drop across the mount** = it's isolating, and the engine itself is shaking more than it should → back to the damper.
**Frame almost as bad as the engine** = the mount has stopped isolating → the mount is the fault.

Those two tests together will tell you whether the engine is making too much vibration, or the mounts are failing to stop normal vibration reaching you.

# And tomorrow morning — the test that actually settles it

**This one is decisive, it's free, and it has never been done.**

The AFR dither is a **catalyst-control function. It only runs in closed loop.** A cold engine runs **open loop** — the computer ignores the oxygen sensors entirely and fuels from a table, with no dither at all.

**So: start it cold, and capture from the first crank for 3–4 minutes.**

**`Engine RPM`** + **`Fuel/Air commanded equivalence ratio`**

| What you see | What it proves |
|---|---|
| **rpm steady while the commanded ratio is flat, then the hunt appears when the dither starts** | **The theory is confirmed.** The PCM's own catalyst dither is driving the rpm oscillation. |
| **rpm hunting at 3.4 s from cold, before any dither** | **The theory is wrong.** Something else is disturbing the engine and the PCM is reacting to it. |

There is no ambiguity in that outcome, and no phase-reading by eye required. It just needs the engine to sit overnight.

| | First night | **Tonight** |
|---|---|---|
| Levels | 14.41 ↔ 14.86 | **14.42 ↔ 14.86** |

**And the shape tells you what it is.** A control loop that's *hunting* produces a rounded, continuous wave. **This is the computer toggling between two fixed setpoints on a timer.** That's a deliberate, programmed function — the catalyst dither — not a loop losing control.

## 2. RPM follows it, in the right direction

| Commanded ratio goes rich (14.42) | RPM peaks |
|---|---|
| 37:01.5 | **~37:03** |
| 37:05 | **~37:06.5** |
| 37:08.5 | **~37:10** |

**RPM peaks about 1–1.5 seconds after each rich command.** Richer mixture, more torque, engine speeds up — with the lag you'd expect from combustion and flywheel inertia at idle.

That's the direction the theory predicts: **command leads, rpm follows.** Not every window reads that cleanly, and reading phase by eye in a closed loop is still not proof — but it's the best evidence yet and it points the right way.

## 3. Throttle: still completely static

`Throttle Position Actually` sits at **min = avg = max**, one flat line, while rpm swings 40–56. It steps between 9.44 / 9.55 / 9.88 / 10.33 over *tens of seconds* — drift, not oscillation.

**Confirmed: the computer never moves the throttle at idle. It holds idle with spark alone.**

## 4. But here's the part that changes my reading

Everything about this oscillation is **consistent with a completely healthy system:**

- Mode 06 says both oxygen sensors respond in **0.014 s** against a 0.4 s limit. Not lazy — so the loop isn't slow because a sensor is failing.
- Mode 06 says both catalysts are at **44 % of their limit** — good oxygen storage.
- **Good storage makes the fore/aft loop slow.** A 3.5-second period is what *healthy* converters produce. A degraded one would make it run *faster*.
- ±1.5 % commanded AFR is within what many Ford PCMs run.

**So the answer to "is the ECU manipulating the rpm?" is yes — deliberately, by design, and everything about how it's doing it looks normal.**

**Yes — the computer is manipulating the rpm.** Confirmed, and now measured better than before:

===== [261] USER 2026-09-05T01:59:01.570Z =====
what app is used to measure vibration hertez

===== [262] ASSISTANT 2026-09-05T01:59:39.080Z =====
**phyphox** — that's the one to use. It's free, made by RWTH Aachen University for physics teaching, no ads, and it has exactly what you need built in.

*(I can't check the Play Store from here, so I'm going on what the app does rather than confirming today's listing.)*

## What to open in it

Once installed, pick the experiment called **"Acceleration Spectrum"** (may be listed under Mechanics). That runs an FFT on the phone's accelerometer and **plots amplitude against frequency in Hz directly** — which is exactly the reading you want.

There's also **"Acceleration (without g)"** if you want the raw waveform over time.

Two useful extras:
- **Export to CSV** — you can send me the file and I'll analyse it properly instead of us reading a screen.
- **Remote access over WiFi** — put the phone on the seat and watch the live spectrum from another device. Useful, since you can't look at the screen while feeling the seat.

**Backup option if phyphox gives you trouble:** *Physics Toolbox Sensor Suite* by Vieyra Software. Same idea, also free.

## How to place it

- **Hard surface beats soft.** A seat cushion absorbs vibration and will lie to you. Best spots: the **seat frame or rail**, the **floor pan**, or wedged firmly against the seat base.
- **Screen up, phone flat, and don't let it slide.** A phone free to move adds its own noise.
- **Record 30–60 seconds.** Longer recording = finer frequency resolution.
- **Record with the engine OFF first** for 30 seconds. That's your noise floor — anything in that recording isn't the engine.

## What you're looking for at ~650 rpm

| Frequency | Order | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **~5.4 Hz** | half | **One cylinder differing from the other five** |
| **~10.8 Hz** | first | **Rotational imbalance, or the engine rocking on its mounts** |
| ~21.7 Hz | second | |
| **~32.5 Hz** | third | **Normal V6 firing pulse.** If this is the big one, the engine is fine and it's isolation. |

**Write down the actual rpm when you record**, because every one of those numbers scales with it. At 660 rpm first order is 11.0 Hz, not 10.8.

## The trick that identifies them for certain

**Record at two different engine speeds** — idle at ~650, then held at ~1000.

| Peak behaviour | What it is |
|---|---|
| **Moves up proportionally with rpm** | **An engine order.** Real vibration from the rotating engine. |
| **Stays at the same frequency** | **A structural resonance** — a bracket, a heat shield, a pipe, a mount. It rings at its own note regardless of engine speed. |

That single comparison separates "the engine is shaking" from "something is resonating," and nothing else you can do makes that distinction.

## Then the comparison that matters

Same phone, same spot, same settings:

1. **Park** — where it shakes
2. **Drive, foot on brake** — where it doesn't

**Whatever peak is big in Park and small in Drive is your vibration.** Send me both spectra, or the CSV files, and I'll tell you which order it is.

## Now — what to actually fix

**Straight answer: nothing on the engine. Stop spending money there.**

You've proven it healthy by every measurement the truck can produce — Mode 06 per-cylinder, both catalysts, all four sensors, cam phasers, fuel cut on both banks, 96 % breathing at wide throttle, trims at −0.78 on both sides. **There is nothing left to fix in the engine.**

### And here's a point I should have raised earlier

**Your symptom has been identical since the day you bought the truck.**

Parts that fail get *worse*. A harmonic balancer that delaminates, a mount that collapses — those degrade over time and you'd have felt it change. Yours hasn't changed in your entire ownership.

That means it's one of two things:

1. **Something that was already worn when you bought it** and has been stable since — twelve-year-old mounts, most likely.
2. **It's what this truck is.** A 60° V6 in a bare regular cab with no sound deadening, at the lowest-load condition it ever sees.

**I cannot distinguish those two from your truck alone.** Nothing measured tonight can.

### So, in order

**1. The control sample — free, and it may end this entirely.**

Find any 2011–2014 F-150 or Mustang with the 3.7. Sit in it at warm idle in Park. **Two minutes.** If it feels the same as yours, you're done — you've been chasing normal, and the purge valve you already fixed was the only real fault there was.

If it's clearly smoother, then yours has a problem and it's worth spending on.

**Do this before buying any part.**

**2. The touch test and the balancer — free, ten minutes each.**

Hand on the engine, then the frame rail beside the mount, then the cab floor. And look at the crank pulley while it idles — a good damper spins true, a failed one visibly wobbles.

**3. Then, and only then, spend money.** If the frequency comes back at ~10.8 Hz and the touch test says the frame is shaking nearly as hard as the engine, **replace the engine mounts.** That's the fix. If the balancer wobbles, replace that first — it's cheaper.

**Do not** replace mounts on suspicion alone. They're not cheap, and you've already had six repairs that changed nothing.

---

## What you achieved tonight, for the record

You found a real fault nobody else found — **the purge valve was leaking unmetered air at idle.** You proved it with the trim slope before the repair, fixed it, and proved it gone after. **That took the shake out of D and R.**

Six previous repairs moved nothing. That one did.

is there another leak ?

===== [266] ASSISTANT 2026-09-05T02:05:37.527Z =====
**No. There is no second leak.**

I can answer that definitively, because fuel trim *is* the leak detector, and it's now clean at the exact condition where the symptom lives.

| Warm idle, in Park | Bank 1 | Bank 2 |
|---|---|---|
| **Before the purge valve** | **+3.13 %** | **+2.34 %** |
| **After, relearned** | **−0.78 %** | **−0.78 %** |

**A leak forces trim positive** — the computer has to add fuel to make up for air it didn't measure. Both banks are now slightly **negative**, identical to each other, and flat.

Three things make that conclusive:

**1. The slope is gone.** Before, trim read +3.13 at idle and 0 % at 2000 rpm — that fade with airflow is the fingerprint of a fixed-size hole. There's now no positive trim at *any* load for a slope to exist in.

**2. Both banks read the same number.** Not close — the same. Leaks are almost never that symmetric.

**3. Cruise trims agree**: −1.56 / −0.78. Negative everywhere.

If anything the engine now runs a whisker *rich* at idle, which is your new purge valve doing its job — delivering fuel vapour from a canister that had years to fill up while the old valve kept it scavenged dry.

## So don't spend on any of this

All of these came off the list when that trim went negative:

- ~~PCV valve, hose, grommet, elbow~~
- ~~Brake booster line and check valve~~
- ~~Intake manifold gasket~~
- ~~Throttle body gasket~~
- ~~Injector O-rings~~
- ~~Smoke test~~

**Every one of them was only ever a candidate to explain a lean bias. There is no lean bias left.** A smoke test now would only find leaks too small to matter.

## One thing that isn't a gap

The **Evaporative System** monitor still reads "Not completed." That's the only test that hasn't run — but it checks the **fuel tank and charcoal canister** for vapour leaks, which is an emissions matter (a loose fuel cap, a cracked canister hose). It sits behind the purge valve when that valve is shut, so **it cannot affect how the engine idles.**

It'll complete on its own once the fuel level and cold-soak conditions happen to be right. Nothing to chase.

**The air side is closed. The fuel side is closed. The engine is closed.** What's left is the vibration path — and that costs nothing to investigate: the control sample, the touch test, and the balancer.
